is the random string drawn in the correlated sampling algorithm.

Algorithm 2

Transformation from one-way perfectly generalizing algorithm to replicable algorithm

The key idea is that correlated sampling converts total variation distance into collision probability, which is the notion that is used in the definition of replicability.

We now use this to prove the main theorem of this section.

Theorem 3.17
.

Fix
m\in\mathbb{N}
and
\beta,\varepsilon,\delta\in(0,1)
. Let
A:\mathcal{X}^{m}\to\mathcal{Y}
be a
(\beta,\varepsilon,\delta)
-one-way perfectly generalizing algorithm with finite output space. Then, for any distribution
D
over
\mathcal{X}
, if
S\sim D^{m}
, Algorithm
2
when run on dataset
S
and with access to
\cal{A}
is
4(\beta+2\varepsilon+\delta)
-replicable.

Proof.

By Lemma
3.8
, we have that
\mathcal{A}
is also
(\beta,0,2\varepsilon+\delta)
-perfectly generalizing. For any distribution
D
over
\mathcal{X}
, let
Sim_{D}
be the canonical distribution witnessing the perfect generalization property. Then, by the definition of
(0,2\varepsilon+\delta)
-indistinguishability, we have that with probability at least
1-\beta
over a draw of a random dataset
S\sim D^{m}
,

d_{TV}(\mathcal{A}(S),Sim_{D})\leq 2\varepsilon+\delta.

From the guarantee of correlated sampling, we have that

\mathbf{Pr}_{r^{\prime}\sim R}[CS(Q_{S},\mathcal{Y},r^{\prime})\neq CS(Sim_{D},\mathcal{Y},r^{\prime})]\leq 2d_{TV}(Q_{S},Sim_{D}).

Using the bound on TV distance from perfect generalization, we get that with probability at least
1-2\beta
over the draw of two datasets
S_{1},S_{2}\sim D^{m}
, we have that

\mathbf{Pr}_{r^{\prime}\sim R}[CS(Q_{S_{1}},\mathcal{Y},r^{\prime})\neq CS(Sim_{D},\mathcal{Y},r^{\prime})]\leq 2(2\varepsilon+\delta)

and

\mathbf{Pr}_{r^{\prime}\sim R}[CS(Q_{S_{2}},\mathcal{Y},r^{\prime})\neq CS(Sim_{D},\mathcal{Y},r^{\prime})]\leq 2(2\varepsilon+\delta).

Consider the event
CS(Q_{S_{1}},\mathcal{Y},r^{\prime})\neq CS(Q_{S_{2}},\mathcal{Y},r^{\prime})
. It is clear that this implies that either
CS(Q_{S_{1}},\mathcal{Y},r^{\prime})\neq CS(Sim_{D},\mathcal{Y},r^{\prime})
or
CS(Q_{S_{2}},\mathcal{Y},r^{\prime})\neq CS(Sim_{D},\mathcal{Y},r^{\prime})
. Hence, we can write that with probability
1-2\beta
over draws of
S_{1}
and
S_{2}
,

\mathbf{Pr}_{r^{\prime}\sim R}[CS(Q_{S_{1}},\mathcal{Y},r^{\prime})\neq CS(Q_{S_{2}},\mathcal{Y},r^{\prime})]\leq 4(2\varepsilon+\delta).

Taking the expectation with respect to the draws of
S_{1}
and
S_{2}
gives us

\mathbf{Pr}_{r^{\prime}\sim R,S_{1},S_{2}\sim D^{m}}[CS(Q_{S_{1}},\mathcal{Y},r^{\prime})\neq CS(Q_{S_{2}},\mathcal{Y},r^{\prime})]\leq 4(2\varepsilon+\delta+\beta)

which proves the result.
∎

Combining the above result and Corollary
3.16
, we get a transformation from approximate differentially private algorithms to replicable algorithms.

Corollary 3.18
.

Fix
m\in\mathbb{N}
, sufficiently small
\rho\in(0,1)
. Let
\varepsilon=\frac{\rho}{\sqrt{8m\log(1/\rho)}}
,
\delta\leq\frac{\varepsilon\rho^{6}}{m^{2}}
. Let
\mathcal{A}:\mathcal{X}^{m}\to\mathcal{Y}
be an
(\varepsilon,\delta)
-DP algorithm with finite output space. Fix a distribution
D
over
\mathcal{X}
, and let
S\sim D^{m}
. Then, Algorithm
2
run with inputs
S
and algorithm
\mathcal{A}
is
O(\rho)
-replicable. Additionally, on any fixed dataset, the output distribution of Algorithm
2
is the same as that of

\mathcal{A}
.

Proof.

From Corollary
3.16
, we have that Algorithm
\mathcal{A}
is
(O(\rho),O(\rho),O(\rho))
-one-way perfectly generalizing. Then, applying Theorem
3.17
proves that the transformation in Algorithm
2
gives a
O(\rho)
-replicable algorithm. Correlated sampling does not change the marginal distribution of the algorithm applied to a dataset and hence the second part of the corollary is proved.
∎

3.4
Replicability Implies Perfect Generalization

In this section, we show how to convert a replicable algorithm to a perfectly generalizing algorithm at a poly-logarithmic cost in
1/\delta
(where
\delta
is the additive perfect generalization parameter).

It’s straightforward to show that
(\delta,\delta)
-replicability can be used to obtain
(O(\delta),0,O(\delta))
-perfect generalization by translating from collision probability to total variation distance. However, since we typically want
\delta
to be very small (often inverse polynomial in the number of samples
m)
, obtaining such small parameters starting from, say,
0.1
-replicability comes at a significant cost. This is because amplifying
0.1
-replicability to
(\delta,\delta)
-replicability incurs a multiplicative sample complexity overhead of
O(1/\delta^{2})
, which is tight by known lower bounds for replicability
[
48
, Theorem 7.1]
, and prohibitively large for many applications. For example, our lower bounds showing tasks where replicability has quadratically higher sample cost than differential privacy (see Section
5
) follow from proving such lower bounds on perfectly generalizing algorithms with
\delta
polynomially small in the dataset size, and then applying our conversion from replicability to perfect generalization. If such a conversion required
1/\delta^{2}
samples, then this would not give us any non-trivial lower bounds on the sample cost associated with replicably solving these problems.

However, this idea still leaves hope, because it achieves
\varepsilon=0
. Hence, by settling for larger
\varepsilon
, we hope to avoid this problem.

Our approach is inspired by a natural attempt to amplify weak replicability parameters into strong parameters. Suppose we wish to turn a
(0.01,0.01)
-replicable algorithm
A
into a
(0.01,\delta)
-replicable one. We know that with probability at least
0.99
over the choice of the randomness
r
for
A
, there is a canonical output
z
such that
A(S;r)=z
with high probability over the sample
S
. Consider running
A
using
k=O(\log(1/\delta))
independent sequences of coin tosses,
r_{1},\dots,r_{k}
, then with probability
1-\delta
, at least one of these sequences will have such a canonical output. Moreover, when such a canonical output exists, we can identify it by running
A(\cdot;r_{j})
on many independent
samples

S
and choosing the plurality outcome if it appears enough times. Unfortunately, there is an obstacle here to directly designing a replicable algorithm. The problem is that there may be many good sequences of coin tosses, each with their own canonical outputs, and it is unclear how to replicably identify a single one.

By relaxing our goal to achieving perfect generalization instead of replicability, we can instead use the exponential mechanism to
sample
from the set of plurality outcomes. We define the score of the plurality output
c_{j}
for coin
r_{j}
to be the number of datasets
S
on which
A(S;r_{j})=c_{j}
, and sample such a
c_{j}
with probability proportional to exponential in its score. We are able to show that the resulting algorithm is
(\delta,\varepsilon,\delta)
-perfectly generalizing with
\varepsilon>0
, but there are several technical nuances that make our analysis not quite straightforward from the standard guarantees of the exponential mechanism. For instance, we need to deal with the fact that the sets of plurality outputs could differ when our algorithm is run on two i.i.d. datasets drawn from the same distribution. Another interesting feature of this proof is that unlike standard uses of the exponential mechanism to obtain differential privacy or perfect generalization, we need to invoke the
accuracy
of the exponential mechanism in our proof of perfect generalization.

Input:
Sample access to distribution
D
, description of
(0.01,0.01)
-replicable algorithm
\mathcal{A}:\mathcal{X}^{*}\to\mathcal{Y}
, sample complexity parameter
m
, perfect generalization parameters
\varepsilon,\delta,\beta

Output:

y\in\mathcal{Y}

1:

Let
k=O(\log(1/\delta))
, and
t=O\left(\frac{\log^{4}(1/\beta)\log(1/\varepsilon)}{\varepsilon^{2}}\right)
.

2:

Draw uniformly random coins
r_{1},r_{2},\dots,r_{k}
for algorithm
\mathcal{A}
.

3:

Draw
k
sets
\mathbf{S_{i}}
, each of
t
samples
S_{i,j}\sim D^{m}
.

4:

for
all
j\in[k]

do

5:

for
all
i\in[t]

do

6:

Run
\mathcal{A}
with coins
r_{j}
and sample
S_{i,j}
to get output
z_{i,j}
.

7:

end

for

8:

Let
c_{j}=\arg\max_{z\in\mathcal{Y}}\sum_{i=1}^{t}\mathbbm{1}[z_{i,j}=z]
, and let
\operatorname{score}\left((j,c_{j}),(\mathbf{S}_{1},\mathbf{S}_{2},\dots,\mathbf{S}_{k})\right)=\sum_{i=1}^{t}\mathbbm{1}[z_{i,j}=c_{j}]
.

9:

end

for

10:

Let
C=\{(1,c_{1}),\dots,(k,c_{k})\}
. Run the exponential mechanism on the set
C
with the score function
\operatorname{score}(.,.)
, sensitivity parameter
4\sqrt{t\log(8kt/\beta)}
, and privacy parameter
\varepsilon
to get value
(j^{*},c_{j^{*}})
.

11:

return
output
c_{j^{*}}
of the previous step.

Algorithm 3

Transformation from replicable algorithm
\mathcal{A}
to perfectly generalizing algorithm
\mathcal{A}^{\prime}

We prove that the above algorithm is sample perfectly generalizing. Note that this can be converted to a perfectly generalizing algorithm with asymptotically the same parameters (for both perfect generalization and accuracy) by setting the
\delta
parameter to be
\delta^{2}
instead and invoking Lemma
3.6
.

Theorem 3.19
.

Fix sufficiently small
\delta,\gamma>0
and
0<\varepsilon\leq 1
. Every
(0.01,0.01)
-replicable algorithm
\mathcal{A}
with
m
samples that succeeds on a statistical task with probability at least
1-\gamma^{2}
can be converted to a
(2\delta,\varepsilon,2\delta)
-sample perfectly generalizing algorithm
\mathcal{A}^{\prime}
taking
O\left(\frac{m\log(1/\varepsilon)}{\varepsilon^{2}}\poly\log(1/\delta)\right)
samples, that succeeds on the statistical task with probability at least
1-O(\delta)-\gamma\log(1/\delta)
.

Proof.

Fix a distribution
D
over the input set
\mathcal{X}
. Our proof is constructive; the corresponding algorithm is given in Algorithm
3
(
\mathcal{A}^{\prime}
), and we feed it with the following inputs: a description of algorithm
\mathcal{A}
, sample complexity parameters
m
, and perfect generalization parameters
(\delta,\varepsilon,\delta)
. (We also give it sample access to distribution
D
). We will start by proving sample perfect generalization of Algorithm
3
.

Claim 3.20
.

Algorithm
\mathcal{A}^{\prime}
(represented in Algorithm
3
) with the input parameters specified in the previous paragraph is
(2\delta,\varepsilon,2\delta)
-sample perfectly generalizing.

Proof.

Consider two samples
S
and
S^{\prime}
drawn independently from
D^{mkt}
. We consider Algorithm
3
run on both of these samples and argue that their output distributions are close in the sense required by sample perfect generalization.

Step 1: At least one coin sequence is good w.h.p.

We say that a choice of the random coin tosses of Algorithm
\mathcal{A}
is “good” if it has a
0.99
-canonical output and call it “bad” otherwise. Then by the two parameter definition of replicability, a random coin sequence is “bad” with probability at most
0.01
. Hence, the probability that all
k
coins sequences drawn in Step
2
of Algorithm
3
are bad is at most
(0.01)^{k}\leq\delta^{2}
for
k=O(\log(1/\delta))
. Let
E_{coin}
represent the event that there is at least one good coin. We will now condition on
E_{coin}
occurring; fix any set of coins
r_{1},\dots,r_{k}
that has non-zero probability of occurring under this conditioning. We will first consider
\mathcal{A}
run on the two independent datasets
S
and
S^{\prime}
with the same random coins fixed above.

Step 2: Empirical output frequencies are close on two independent datasets.

We define stage
j
of Algorithm
3
as the process involved in generating
c_{j}
(i.e., one iteration of the outer loop in Step
4
). We now use uniform convergence to argue that with high probability over the samples, the empirical frequencies of the outputs of all stages, i.e. all values of
Score\left((j,c_{j}),(\mathbf{S}_{1},\mathbf{S}_{2},\dots,\mathbf{S}_{k})\right)
, are close to their expectation.

Start by defining
H
to be the function class consisting of point functions, i.e., functions of the form
h_{\ell}(x)=1
if
x={\ell}
, and
h_{\ell}(x)=0
otherwise, for every
\ell\in\mathcal{Y}
. It is easy to prove that the VC dimension of
H
is equal to
1
. Let
Q_{j}
be the distribution of the output of the replicable algorithm
\mathcal{A}
when run with coin
r_{j}
on a random sample.

Then, using uniform convergence (Theorem
2.16
), we get for every fixed
j
and for every
\gamma>0
, that

\mathbf{Pr}_{z_{1,j},\dots,z_{t,j}\sim Q_{j}}\left[\sup_{h_{\ell}\in H}\left|\frac{1}{t}\sum_{i=1}^{t}\mathbbm{1}[h_{\ell}(z_{i,j})=1]-\mathbf{Pr}_{z\sim Q_{j}}[h_{\ell}(z)=1]\right|\geq\gamma\right]\leq 8te^{-\gamma^{2}t/8}.

Observe that
\frac{1}{t}\sum_{i=1}^{t}\mathbbm{1}[h_{\ell}(z_{i,j})=1]=\frac{1}{t}\sum_{i=1}^{t}\mathbbm{1}[z_{i,j}=\ell]
. Similarly,
\mathbf{Pr}_{z\sim Q}[h_{\ell}(z)=1]=\mathbf{Pr}_{z\sim Q_{j}}[z=\ell]
. Hence, we get that

\mathbf{Pr}_{z_{1,j},\dots,z_{t,j}\sim Q_{j}}\left[\sup_{\ell\in\mathcal{Y}}\left|\frac{1}{t}\sum_{i=1}^{t}\mathbbm{1}[z_{i,j}=\ell]-\mathbf{Pr}_{z\sim Q_{j}}[z=\ell]\right|\geq\gamma\right]\leq 8te^{-\gamma^{2}t/8}.

Setting
\gamma=2\sqrt{\frac{\log(8kt/\delta)}{t}}
, we get that

\mathbf{Pr}_{z_{1,j},\dots,z_{t,j}\sim Q_{j}}\left[\sup_{\ell\in\mathcal{Y}}\left|\sum_{i=1}^{t}\mathbbm{1}[z_{i,j}=\ell]-t\mathbf{Pr}_{z\sim Q_{j}}[z=\ell]\right|\geq 2\sqrt{t\log(8kt/\delta)}\right]\leq\frac{\delta^{2}}{2k}.

Using a union bound over all
k
stages of the algorithm, this guarantees us that the empirical frequencies (and in particular, the values
\operatorname{score}\left((j,c_{j}),(\mathbf{S}_{1},\mathbf{S}_{2},\dots,\mathbf{S}_{k})\right)
are all within
2\sqrt{t\log(8kt/\delta)}
of their expectations with probability at least
1-\delta^{2}/2
.

Note that since we conditioned on a fixed random coin sequence, all the randomness in
z_{i,j}
comes from the data sample. Hence, if we consider another sample
S^{\prime}=(\mathbf{S}^{\prime}_{1},\mathbf{S}^{\prime}_{2},\dots,\mathbf{S}^{\prime}_{k})
drawn i.i.d .from
D^{mkt}
, we have that with probability at least
1-\delta^{2}
over the draws of
S
and
S^{\prime}
that for all
j\in[k]
and all
\ell\in\mathcal{Y}
that
\operatorname{score}\left((j,\ell),(\mathbf{S}_{1},\mathbf{S}_{2},\dots,\mathbf{S}_{k})\right)
and
\operatorname{score}\left((j,\ell),(\mathbf{S}^{\prime}_{1},\mathbf{S}^{\prime}_{2},\dots,\mathbf{S}^{\prime}_{k})\right)
are both within
2\sqrt{t\log(8kt/\delta)}
of their expectations, and are hence within
4\sqrt{t\log(8kt/\delta)}
of each other. We call this event
E_{sample}
, and fix any sample pairs
(S,S^{\prime})
that occur with non-zero probability conditioned on this event. This allows us to argue that with probability at least
1-\delta^{2}
, for all
j\in[k]
,

\displaystyle|\operatorname{score}\left((j,c_{j}),(\mathbf{S}_{1},\mathbf{S}_{2},\dots,\mathbf{S}_{k})\right)-\operatorname{score}\left((j,c^{\prime}_{j}),(\mathbf{S}^{\prime}_{1},\mathbf{S}^{\prime}_{2},\dots,\mathbf{S}^{\prime}_{k})\right)|\leq 4\sqrt{t\log(8kt/\delta)}.

(6)

This follows directly from the above argument if
c_{j}=c^{\prime}_{j}
, but if they are not equal, it also holds since otherwise either
c_{j}
or
c^{\prime}_{j}
would not be a plurality output in stage
j
of the corresponding runs (since there would be an output that occurs more times in stage
j
). This is because uniform convergence guarantees us that if
c_{j}
occurs
a
times in stage
j
when the algorithm is run on dataset
S
, then
c_{j}
occurs atleast
a-4\sqrt{t\log(8kt/\delta)}
times in stage
j
when the algorithm is run on dataset
S^{\prime}
. Hence, if
c^{\prime}_{j}
occurs less than
a-4\sqrt{t\log(8kt/\delta)}
in stage
j
, then we’d get that
c_{j}
would be the plurality output of stage
j
in the run on dataset
S^{\prime}
and not
c^{\prime}_{j}
, which is a contradiction. This shows that
\operatorname{score}\left((j,c_{j}),(\mathbf{S}_{1},\mathbf{S}_{2},\dots,\mathbf{S}_{k})\right)-\operatorname{score}\left((j,c^{\prime}_{j}),(\mathbf{S}^{\prime}_{1},\mathbf{S}^{\prime}_{2},\dots,\mathbf{S}^{\prime}_{k})\right)\leq 4\sqrt{t\log(8kt/\delta)}
; the other direction can be proved similarly.

Step 3: Arguing that there is at least one canonical output
c_{j}
with high score.

Conditioned on
E_{coin}
, we know that the run of Algorithm
3
on
S
has at least one coin sequence with a
0.99
-canonical output
z
. Suppose
r_{j}
is such a coin sequence. From the settings of
k
and
t
, we get that
2\sqrt{t\log(8kt/\delta)}\leq 0.09t
. Hence, conditioned further on
E_{sample}
, we know that this canonical output
z
is equal to the plurality output
c_{j}
in stage
j
, and that
\operatorname{score}\left((j,c_{j}),(\mathbf{S}_{1},\mathbf{S}_{2},\dots,\mathbf{S}_{k})\right)
is at least
0.9t
. Hence, there exists an candidate
(j,c_{j})
with score at least
0.9t
.

Step 4: Arguing that probable outputs
(j^{*},c_{j^{*}})
are in both output sets
C
and
C^{\prime}
.

By the accuracy guarantee of the exponential mechanism (Lemma
2.7
), we have that

\mathbf{Pr}\left[\max_{j\in[k]}\operatorname{score}\left((j,c_{j}),(\mathbf{S}_{1},\mathbf{S}_{2},\dots,\mathbf{S}_{k})\right)-\operatorname{score}\left((j^{*},c_{j^{*}}),(\mathbf{S}_{1},\mathbf{S}_{2},\dots,\mathbf{S}_{k})\right)\geq 2\Delta\frac{\ln k+k}{\varepsilon}\right]\leq e^{-k}=\delta,

where
\Delta=4\sqrt{t\log(8kt/\delta)}
. Hence, for the settings of
t
and
k
, we get that
2\Delta\frac{\ln k+k}{\varepsilon}=O(\frac{k\sqrt{t\log(8kt/\delta)}}{\varepsilon})=o(t)
. Hence, we have that

\mathbf{Pr}\left[\operatorname{score}\left((j^{*},c_{j^{*}}),(\mathbf{S}_{1},\mathbf{S}_{2},\dots,\mathbf{S}_{k})\right)\geq 0.9t-2\Delta\frac{\ln k+k}{\varepsilon}\right]\leq e^{-k}=\delta,

which implies that for sufficiently small
\delta
,

\mathbf{Pr}\left[\operatorname{score}\left((j^{*},c_{j^{*}}),(\mathbf{S}_{1},\mathbf{S}_{2},\dots,\mathbf{S}_{k})\right)\geq 0.8t\right]\leq e^{-k}=\delta.

Let’s consider any such
c_{j^{*}}
. By the conditioning on
E_{sample}
, we have that
c_{j^{*}}
occurs more than
0.8t-4\sqrt{t\log(kt/\delta)}>0.5t
times in the output set of stage
j^{*}
when Algorithm
3
is run on the sample
S^{\prime}
. Hence,
c^{\prime}_{j^{*}}
is also equal to
c_{j^{*}}
.

Step 5: Proving that
\mathcal{A}^{\prime}(S,r)\approx_{\varepsilon,\delta}\mathcal{A}^{\prime}(S^{\prime},r)
w.h.p.

We exploit the fact that two random variables
C
and
D
are
(\varepsilon,\delta)
-indistinguishable if w.p.
\geq 1-\delta
over a draw
o
from the distribution of
C
, we have
e^{-\varepsilon}\mathbf{Pr}[D=o]\leq\mathbf{Pr}[C=o]\leq e^{\varepsilon}\mathbf{Pr}[D=o]
, and vice versa for a draw from the distribution of
D

[
55
, Lemma 3.3, Part 1]
.

We proved in Step
4
that fixing any coins and sample pairs that have non-zero probability of occurring conditioned on
E_{coin}
and
E_{sample}
, with probability at least
1-\delta
from a draw of
A^{\prime}(S,r)
(where the randomness is only that of the exponential mechanism), the output
(j^{*},c_{j^{*}})
occurs in both the sets
C
and
C^{\prime}
. For all such outputs, our idea is to use the differential privacy analysis of the exponential mechanism.

A technical obstacle we need to surmount is that the output sets
C
and
C^{\prime}
might be different, and so the normalizing factors used in the exponential mechanism will vary accordingly. We deal with this by invoking Inequality
6
, which points out that even though the output sets are different, the scores
\operatorname{score}\left((j,c_{j}),(\mathbf{S}_{1},\mathbf{S}_{2},\dots,\mathbf{S}_{k})\right)
and
\operatorname{score}\left((j,c^{\prime}_{j}),(\mathbf{S}^{\prime}_{1},\mathbf{S}^{\prime}_{2},\dots,\mathbf{S}^{\prime}_{k})\right)
can differ by at most the sensitivity specified in Step
10
where the exponential mechanism is invoked.

Hence, exactly mimicking the differential privacy analysis of the exponential mechanism (see e.g.,
[
37
]
, Theorem 3.10) conditioned on
E_{coin}
and
E_{sample}
, with probability at least
1-\delta
from a draw
(j^{*},c^{\prime}_{j^{*}})
of
\mathcal{A}^{\prime}(S,r)
, we get that

e^{-\varepsilon}\mathbf{Pr}[\mathcal{A}^{\prime}(S,r)=(j^{*},c_{j^{*}})]\leq\mathbf{Pr}[\mathcal{A}^{\prime}(S^{\prime},r)=(j^{*},c^{\prime}_{j^{*}})]\leq e^{\varepsilon}\mathbf{Pr}(\mathcal{A}^{\prime}(S,r)=(j^{*},c_{j^{*}})]

and, moreover,
c^{\prime}_{j^{*}}=c_{j^{*}}
. By symmetry (since
S
and
S^{\prime}
are both independent samples from the distribution with the same properties), conditioned on
E_{coin}
and
E_{sample}
, we get that with probability at least
1-\delta
from a draw
(j^{*},c_{j^{*}})
of
\mathcal{A}^{\prime}(S^{\prime},r)
,

e^{-\varepsilon}\mathbf{Pr}[\mathcal{A}^{\prime}(S,r)=(j^{*},c_{j^{*}})]\leq\mathbf{Pr}[\mathcal{A}^{\prime}(S^{\prime},r)=(j^{*},c_{j^{*}})]\leq e^{\varepsilon}\mathbf{Pr}[\mathcal{A}^{\prime}(S,r)=(j^{*},c_{j^{*}})].

Hence, we have proved that conditioned on any fixed coins and sample pairs with non-zero probability of occurring conditioned on
E_{coin}
and
E_{sample}
, we have
\mathcal{A}^{\prime}(S,r)\approx_{\varepsilon,\delta}\mathcal{A}^{\prime}(S^{\prime},r)
. Now, using the law of total probability, we get that

\displaystyle\mathbf{Pr}_{r_{1},\dots,r_{k}}\left[\mathbf{Pr}_{S,S^{\prime}\sim D^{mkt}}\Big[\mathcal{A}^{\prime}(S;r_{1},\dots,r_{k})\approx_{\varepsilon,\delta}\mathcal{A}^{\prime}(S^{\prime};r_{1},\dots,r_{k})\Big]\geq 1-\delta^{2}\right]\geq 1-\delta^{2}.

(7)

Step 6: Switch quantifiers to get sample perfect generalization:

Now, we switch the quantifiers in equation
7
.

\displaystyle\mathbf{Pr}_{S,S^{\prime}\sim D^{mkt},r_{1},\dots,r_{k}}\Big[\mathcal{A}^{\prime}(S;r_{1},\dots,r_{k})\approx_{\varepsilon,\delta}\mathcal{A}^{\prime}(S^{\prime};r_{1},\dots,r_{k})\Big]\geq 1-2\delta^{2}

\displaystyle\implies

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{S,S^{\prime}\sim D^{mkt}}\left[\mathbf{Pr}_{r_{1},\dots,r_{k}}\Big[\mathcal{A}^{\prime}(S;r_{1},\dots,r_{k})\approx_{\varepsilon,\delta}\mathcal{A}^{\prime}(S^{\prime};r_{1},\dots,r_{k})\Big]\right]\geq 1-2\delta^{2}

\displaystyle\implies

\displaystyle\mathbf{Pr}_{S,S^{\prime}\sim D^{mkt}}\left[\mathbf{Pr}_{r_{1},\dots,r_{k}}\Big[\mathcal{A}^{\prime}(S;r_{1},\dots,r_{k})\approx_{\varepsilon,\delta}\mathcal{A}^{\prime}(S^{\prime};r_{1},\dots,r_{k})\Big]\geq d\right]\geq\frac{1-2\delta^{2}-d}{1-d}.

Here, the last inequality holds by the reverse Markov inequality. Setting
d=1-\delta
, we get that

\displaystyle\mathbf{Pr}_{S,S^{\prime}\sim D^{mkt}}\left[\mathbf{Pr}_{r_{1},\dots,r_{k}}\Big[\mathcal{A}^{\prime}(S;r_{1},\dots,r_{k})\approx_{\varepsilon,\delta}\mathcal{A}^{\prime}(S^{\prime};r_{1},\dots,r_{k})\Big]\geq 1-\delta\right]\geq 1-2\delta.

Now, using the fact that if
X\approx_{\varepsilon,\delta}Y
and
M\approx_{\varepsilon,\delta}N
, then
\alpha X+(1-\alpha)M\approx_{\varepsilon,\delta}\alpha Y+(1-\alpha)N
for every
\alpha\in[0,1]
(i.e.,
(\varepsilon,\delta)
-indistinguishability is preserved under convex combinations), we get that

\mathbf{Pr}_{S,S^{\prime}\sim D^{mkt}}\left[\mathcal{A}^{\prime}(S)\approx_{\varepsilon,2\delta}\mathcal{A}^{\prime}(S^{\prime})\right]\geq 1-2\delta.

This proves that
\mathcal{A}^{\prime}
with the specified inputs is
(2\delta,\varepsilon,2\delta)
-sample perfectly generalizing, as required. Next, we deal with accuracy.
∎

Claim 3.21
.

If Algorithm
\mathcal{A}
succeeds at a statistical task with probability at least
1-\gamma^{2}
, Algorithm
\mathcal{A}^{\prime}
succeeds at the same statistical task with probability at least
1-O(\delta)-\gamma\log(1/\delta)
.

Proof.

Recall the definition of success for a statistical task. The statistical task is defined by a set of distribution, set pairs. For every distribution
D
, there is an associated good set of outputs
O_{D}
. An algorithm succeeds at this task with probability at least
1-\gamma
if it outputs a member of this good set with at least that probability (taken over random samples from
D
and any internal coins of the algorithm).

If
\mathcal{A}
succeeds at the task with probability at least
1-\gamma^{2}
, by using reverse Markov’s inequality as in Step 6 of the previous proof, we have that

\displaystyle\mathbf{Pr}_{r}\left[\mathbf{Pr}_{S\sim D^{m}}\Big[\mathcal{A}(S;r)\in O_{D}\Big]\geq 1-\gamma\right]\geq 1-\gamma

(8)

We say a coin sequence
r_{j}
is “accurate” if the inner inequality under the probability is satisfied. Recall that we call a coin sequence
r_{j}
“good” if it has a
0.99
-canonical output. By the analysis in Step
1
of the previous proof, we have that with probability at least
1-\delta^{2}
, there is a good coin sequence among the runs
r_{1},\dots,r_{k}
. Now, the probability that all
k
coin sequences are “accurate” is equal to
(1-\gamma)^{\log(1/\delta)}\geq 1-\gamma\log(1/\delta)
. (This follows from Bernoulli’s inequality
(1+a)^{k}\geq 1+ak
for all
a\geq-1
and non-negative integers
k
.) Hence, by a union bound, the probability that the set of runs
r_{1},\dots,r_{k}
both contains a good coin sequence and that all the coin sequences in the set are accurate is at least
1-\gamma\log(1/\delta)-\delta^{2}
. Call this event
E_{coin-acc}
and condition on it. Additionally, condition on
E_{sample}
as defined in Step
2
of the previous proof. Then, by the analysis in Step 4 of the previous proof, we have that

\mathbf{Pr}\left[\operatorname{score}\left((j^{*},c_{j^{*}}),(\mathbf{S}_{1},\mathbf{S}_{2},\dots,\mathbf{S}_{k})\right)\geq 0.8t\right]\leq e^{-k}=\delta,

which implies that the exponential mechanism outputs a plurality output that occurs at least
0.8t
times in its stage with probability at least
1-\delta
. Since we have conditioned on
E_{sample}
, we have that empirical frequencies are close to their expected values, and hence the exponential mechanism outputs the canonical output of a coin sequence that is at least
0.25
-good with probability at least
1-\delta
. Using the law of total probability to remove the conditioning on
E_{sample}
, we get that the exponential mechanism outputs the canonical output of a coin sequence that is at least
0.25
-good with probability at least
1-\delta-\delta^{2}
(since event
E_{sample}
happens with probability at least
1-\delta^{2}
). Note that since all the drawn coin sequences are accurate, we get that the canonical output for every such sequence is in the good set
O_{D}
(otherwise, the inequality inside the outer probability in equation
8
would not be satisfied). Hence, conditioned on
E_{coin-acc}
, we have that
\mathcal{A}^{\prime}
outputs an element of the good set with probability at least
1-\delta-\delta^{2}
. Using the law of total probability, we then get that
\mathcal{A}^{\prime}
outputs an element of the good set with probability at least
1-\delta-2\delta^{2}-\gamma\log(1/\delta)=1-O(\delta)-\gamma\log(1/\delta)
.
∎

Hence, combining the two claims on perfect generalization and accuracy, we complete the proof of the theorem.
∎

4
Separating Stability: Computational Barriers

In this section, we show that standard cryptographic assumptions imply there cannot exist computationally efficient transformations from differentially private algorithms to replicable ones.
Moreover, such cryptographic assumptions are necessary: if one-way functions do not exist, there exists an efficient algorithm for correlated sampling (and therefore for converting DP to replicability as well via
Corollary

3.18
).

In Section
4.1
, we define
\Pi_{\textit{RandEnc}}
, a statistical promise problem.

Given a public key
\mathbf{pk}
and a dataset of ciphertexts encrypting the same bit
b
under
\mathbf{pk}
, a solution to
\Pi_{\textit{RandEnc}}
is any encryption of
b
under
\mathbf{pk}
.
In Section
4.1.1
, we give a simple algorithm
\mathtt{DPRandEnc}
solving
\Pi_{\textit{RandEnc}}
.
\mathtt{DPRandEnc}
is
(\varepsilon,\delta)
-differentially private and runs in polynomial time.
In Section
4.1.2
, we show that the existence of an efficient replicable algorithm for
\Pi_{\textit{RandEnc}}
would violate the security guarantee of the encryption scheme. Thus, assuming randomizable encryption schemes exist, there is no efficient transformation from DP algorithms to replicable algorithms for
\Pi_{\textit{RandEnc}}
.

\Pi_{\textit{RandEnc}}
can be instantiated with any PKE satisfying the requirements of
Definition

4.2
, but to demonstrate that these requirements are not unreasonable, in Section
4.1.3
we show that they are satisfied by the Goldwasser-Micali public-key encryption scheme
[
43
]
.
Therefore the hardness of quadratic residuosity is sufficient to show hardness for the transformation from differential privacy to replicability.

In
Section

4.2
, we give an algorithm for correlated sampling that is efficient so long as no one-way functions exist. This algorithm can in turn be used in
Algorithm

2
, to implement the correlated sampling step of the transformation from a one-way perfectly generalizing algorithm to a replicable one, giving an efficient transformation.

4.1
Cryptographic Hardness of Replicability

We define a promise problem
\Pi_{\textit{RandEnc}}
(Definition
4.3
) for a public-key encryption scheme.
\Pi_{\textit{RandEnc}}
is parameterized by a public-key
\mathbf{pk}
for a public-key encryption scheme
\mathcal{E}
with message space
\{0,1\}
and ciphertext space
\mathcal{C}
. An instance of
\Pi_{\textit{RandEnc}}
consists of a sample of
m
elements
c_{i}
, drawn i.i.d. from an unknown distribution
D
over
\mathcal{C}
.
Promised that either

1.

D
is supported entirely on encryptions of
0
under
\mathbf{pk}
or

2.

D
is supported entirely on encryptions of
1
under
\mathbf{pk}
,

Problem
\Pi_{\textit{RandEnc}}
asks the algorithm to output an encryption of
0
under
\mathbf{pk}
in the first case and an encryption of
1
under
\mathbf{pk}
in the second case.

We show that if the public-key encryption scheme supports a strong form of rerandomization, Problem
\Pi_{\textit{RandEnc}}
can be efficiently solved with a differentially private algorithm. At the same time,
\Pi_{\textit{RandEnc}}
cannot be efficiently solved using a replicable algorithm, assuming the security of the underlying encryption scheme.
Thus, in this setting, there cannot be an efficient black-box reduction from DP algorithms to replicable algorithms.

For our construction, we use a standard definition for public-key encryption.

Definition 4.1

(Public-Key Encryption Scheme)
.

Let
\lambda\in\mathbb{N}
be a security parameter and
\mathcal{E}=(\mathsf{KeyGen},\mathsf{Enc},\mathsf{Dec})
be a tuple of algorithms running in time
\poly(\lambda)
, with
\mathsf{KeyGen}:1^{*}\rightarrow\mathcal{K}_{p}\times\mathcal{K}_{s}
,
\mathsf{Enc}:\mathcal{K}_{p}\times\{0,1\}\rightarrow\mathcal{C}
, and
\mathsf{Dec}:\mathcal{K}_{s}\times\mathcal{C}\rightarrow\{0,1\}\cup\bot
. We say
\mathcal{E}
is a
public-key encryption scheme
if it has the following properties.

•

Correctness: Let
(\mathbf{sk},\mathbf{pk})\leftarrow\mathsf{KeyGen}(\lambda)
and
c\leftarrow\mathsf{Enc}(\mathbf{pk},b)
for
b\in\{0,1\}
. Then
\mathsf{Dec}(\mathbf{sk},c)=b
.

•

Security: There exists a negligible function
\varepsilon(\lambda)
, such that for all adversaries
\mathcal{A}
running in time
\poly(\lambda)
, letting
(\mathbf{sk},\mathbf{pk})\leftarrow\mathsf{KeyGen}(\lambda)
we have

\left|\mathbf{Pr}[\mathcal{A}(\mathbf{pk},c)=1\mid c\leftarrow\mathsf{Enc}(\mathbf{pk},1)]-\mathbf{Pr}[\mathcal{A}(\mathbf{pk},c)=1\mid c\leftarrow\mathsf{Enc}(\mathbf{pk},0)]\right|<\varepsilon(\lambda).

We also require that a public-key encryption scheme allows for efficient, publicly computable ciphertext verification and rerandomization procedures.

Definition 4.2

(Randomizeable Encryption Scheme)
.

Let
\mathcal{E}=(\mathsf{KeyGen},\mathsf{Enc},\mathsf{Dec})
be a public-key encryption scheme. We call
\mathcal{E}
a
randomizeable encryption scheme
if it supports the following additional procedures.

•

(Perfect) Verification of Ciphertexts:

There exists a deterministic polytime algorithm
\mathcal{V}
such that, for an honestly generated key pair
(\mathbf{sk},\mathbf{pk})\leftarrow\mathsf{KeyGen}(\lambda)
and value
c
,

–

If
\mathsf{Dec}(\mathbf{sk},c)\in\{0,1\}
, then
\mathcal{V}(\mathbf{pk},c)=1

–

If
\mathsf{Dec}(\mathbf{sk},c)=\bot
, then
\mathcal{V}(\mathbf{pk},c)=0

•

(Perfect) Randomization of Ciphertexts:

There exists a randomized polytime algorithm
\mathsf{Rerandomize}
such that,
for all honestly generated key pairs
(\mathbf{sk},\mathbf{pk})\leftarrow\mathsf{KeyGen}(\lambda)
, and all ciphertexts
c_{1},c_{2}
such that
\mathsf{Dec}(\mathbf{sk},c_{1})=\mathsf{Dec}(\mathbf{sk},c_{2})
,

–

d_{TV}(\mathsf{Rerandomize}(\mathbf{pk},c_{1}),\mathsf{Rerandomize}(\mathbf{pk},c_{2}))=0

–

\mathsf{Dec}(\mathbf{sk},\mathsf{Rerandomize}(\mathbf{pk},c))=\mathsf{Dec}(\mathbf{sk},c)

Consider the following search problem
\Pi_{\textit{RandEnc}}
. Given a public key
\mathbf{pk}
for an encryption scheme
\mathcal{E}
, and an i.i.d. sample of
m
elements from a distribution
D
supported on encryptions under
\mathbf{pk}
of a fixed bit
b\in\{0,1\}
, output an encryption of
b
under
\mathbf{pk}
.

Definition 4.3

(Ciphertext Identification Problem)
.

An instance of
\Pi_{\textit{RandEnc}}
is defined as follows.
Let
\mathcal{E}=(\mathsf{KeyGen},\mathsf{Enc},\mathsf{Dec})
be a randomizeable encryption scheme (Definition
4.2
).
Let
\lambda,m\in\mathbb{N}
, and let
D
be a distribution over the ciphertext space
\mathcal{C}
of
\mathcal{E}
.
Given public key
\mathbf{pk}
, honestly generated as
(\mathbf{pk},\mathbf{sk})\leftarrow\mathsf{KeyGen}(\lambda)
, and a sample
S\sim D^{m}
drawn i.i.d. from
D
,
output an element
c^{*}\in\mathcal{C}\cup\bot
such that

1.

If
\mathsf{Dec}(\mathbf{sk},c)=1
for all
c\in\mathtt{supp}(D)
,
\mathsf{Dec}(\mathbf{sk},c^{*})=1

2.

If
\mathsf{Dec}(\mathbf{sk},c)=0
for all
c\in\mathtt{supp}(D)
,
\mathsf{Dec}(\mathbf{sk},c^{*})=0

4.1.1
DP Algorithm for
\Pi_{\textit{RandEnc}}

In this subsection, we present a differentially private algorithm
\mathtt{DPRandEnc}
for
\Pi_{\textit{RandEnc}}
.
Our algorithm removes from the dataset
S
all
c_{i}
for which verification fails, i.e.,
\mathcal{V}(\mathbf{pk},c_{i})=0
. It then pads the remaining elements with
k
encryptions of 0 under
\mathbf{pk}
and
k
encryptions of 1 under
\mathbf{pk}
. An element
c_{i}
from the new dataset is then chosen uniformly at random, and the algorithm outputs
\mathsf{Rerandomize}(\mathbf{pk},c_{i})
.

Padding the dataset with additional ciphertexts, balanced between encryptions of 0 and 1, guarantees privacy by ensuring that exchanging any element of
S
for another will not significantly change the probability that the ciphertext chosen for rerandomization encrypts a particular bit.
If the distribution
D
is supported on one of the two promised distributions,
\mathtt{DPRandEnc}
will be correct unless it chooses to rerandomize an inserted ciphertext which encrypts the incorrect bit. So long as the input sample is of size
m
much larger than
k
, this will happen only with small probability.

Result:

Outputs a ciphertext
c\in\mathcal{C}

Input:
Sample
S
of
m
elements drawn i.i.d. from
D

Parameters:

•

Privacy
\varepsilon
, failure probability
\beta
, padding length
k=\frac{1}{\varepsilon}

•

Sample Complexity
m=m(\varepsilon,\beta)\in O\left(\frac{1}{\varepsilon\beta}\right)

Algorithm:

1.

For
i\in[m]
, remove
c_{i}
from
S
if
\mathcal{V}(\mathbf{pk},c_{i})=0

2.

Add
k
ciphertexts
\mathsf{Enc}(\mathbf{pk},0)
to the dataset

3.

Add
k
ciphertexts
\mathsf{Enc}(\mathbf{pk},1)
to the dataset

4.

Choose
c
uniformly at random from the new dataset

return

\mathsf{Rerandomize}(\mathbf{pk},c)

Algorithm 4

\mathtt{DPRandEnc}

Lemma 4.4
.

Let
\varepsilon,\beta\in(0,1/2)
. Then for
m\in\Omega(1/(\varepsilon\beta))
and
k=1/\varepsilon
,

\Pi_{\textit{RandEnc}}
(
Algorithm

4
) runs in time
\poly(\lambda,1/\varepsilon,1/\beta)
, is
\varepsilon
-DP, and correct except with probability at most
\beta
.

Proof.

We begin by showing
\mathtt{DPRandEnc}
is
\varepsilon
-DP.
Note that the last step of
\mathtt{DPRandEnc}
calls
\mathsf{Rerandomize}
on a ciphertext
c
that is guaranteed to be a valid encryption of a bit
b\in\{0,1\}
, since all inputs failing verification are removed from
S
before
c
is drawn, and only valid ciphertexts under
\mathbf{pk}
are added to the input dataset.

We will bound how much the probability that
c
encrypts a fixed bit
b
can differ across neighboring data sets.
Let
c
be a random variable denoting the ciphertext chosen for rerandomization. For all
b\in\{0,1\}
and neighboring datasets
S,S^{\prime}
, let
S_{b}
,
S_{\neg b}
, and
S_{\bot}
denote the subsets of
S
such that
\mathsf{Dec}(\mathbf{sk},c)=b
,
\mathsf{Dec}(\mathbf{sk},c)=\neg b
, and
\mathsf{Dec}(\mathbf{sk},c)=\bot
respectively, and let
S^{\prime}_{b}
,
S^{\prime}_{\neg b}
, and
S^{\prime}_{\bot}
be defined analogously. Then

\displaystyle\frac{\mathbf{Pr}[\mathsf{Dec}(\mathbf{sk},c)=b\mid S]}{\mathbf{Pr}[\mathsf{Dec}(\mathbf{sk},c)=b\mid S^{\prime}]}

\displaystyle=\frac{|S_{b}|+k}{m-|S_{\bot}|+2k}\cdot\frac{m-|S^{\prime}_{\bot}|+2k}{|S^{\prime}_{b}|+k}

\displaystyle=\frac{|S_{b}|+k}{|S_{b}|+|S_{\neg b}|+2k}\cdot\frac{|S^{\prime}_{b}|+|S^{\prime}_{\neg b}|+2k}{|S^{\prime}_{b}|+k}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{|S_{b}|+k}{|S_{b}|+|S_{\neg b}|+2k}\cdot\frac{|S_{b}|+|S_{\neg b}|+2k}{|S_{b}|-1+k}

\displaystyle=\frac{|S_{b}|+k}{|S_{b}|-1+k}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{k+1}{k},

where the first inequality follows from
S,S^{\prime}
neighboring, and the fact that for
a>b
,
\frac{a}{b-1}>\frac{a+1}{b}
, so assuming
|S_{b}|\geq 1
and
|S^{\prime}_{b}|=|S_{b}|-1
maximizes the rightmost fraction.
Because the output distribution of
\mathsf{Rerandomize}(\mathbf{pk},c)
is the same for all ciphertexts encrypting the same bit under
\mathbf{pk}
, it follows that for all subsets
T\subseteq\mathcal{C}
,

\displaystyle\mathbf{Pr}[\mathtt{DPRandEnc}(S)\in T]

\displaystyle=\mathbf{Pr}[\mathsf{Rerandomize}(\mathbf{pk},c)\in T\mid\mathsf{Dec}(\mathbf{sk},c)=1]\cdot\mathbf{Pr}[\mathsf{Dec}(\mathbf{sk},c)=1\mid S]

\displaystyle\quad\quad\quad+\mathbf{Pr}[\mathsf{Rerandomize}(\mathbf{pk},c)\in T\mid\mathsf{Dec}(\mathbf{sk},c)=0]\cdot\mathbf{Pr}[\mathsf{Dec}(\mathbf{sk},c)=0\mid S].

Using
p_{b}
to denote
\mathbf{Pr}[\mathsf{Rerandomize}(\mathbf{pk},c)\in T\mid\mathsf{Dec}(\mathbf{sk},c)=b]
, for
b\in\{0,1\}
, we then have that

\displaystyle\mathbf{Pr}[\mathtt{DPRandEnc}(S)\in T]

\displaystyle=p_{1}\cdot\mathbf{Pr}[\mathsf{Dec}(\mathbf{sk},c)=1\mid S]+p_{0}\cdot\mathbf{Pr}[\mathsf{Dec}(\mathbf{sk},c)=0\mid S]

\displaystyle\leq\frac{k+1}{k}\left(p_{1}\cdot\mathbf{Pr}[\mathsf{Dec}(\mathbf{sk},c)=1\mid S^{\prime}]+p_{0}\cdot\mathbf{Pr}[\mathsf{Dec}(\mathbf{sk},c)=0\mid S^{\prime}]\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{k+1}{k}\cdot\mathbf{Pr}[\mathtt{DPRandEnc}(S^{\prime})\in T]

\displaystyle\leq e^{\varepsilon}\cdot\mathbf{Pr}[\mathtt{DPRandEnc}(S^{\prime})\in T]

where the final inequality follows from taking
k=1/\varepsilon
and observing
1+x\leq e^{x}
.

It remains to argue correctness of
\mathtt{DPRandEnc}
when the sample
S
is drawn from one of the promised distributions. In this case, the input sample
S
consists of
m
valid encryptions of the same bit
b
under
\mathbf{pk}
. Because
\mathsf{Rerandomize}(\mathbf{pk},c)
is plaintext-preserving, the probability that
\Pi_{\textit{RandEnc}}
is incorrect given
S
, i.e., outputs a ciphertext encrypting
\neg b
, is exactly the probability that one of the inserted ciphertexts encrypting
\neg b
is chosen for rerandomization. This happens with probability
\frac{k}{m+2k}
, so taking
m>k/\beta=1/(\varepsilon\beta)
ensures
\Pi_{\textit{RandEnc}}
is correct except with probability
\beta
.
∎

4.1.2
Cryptographic Adversary from Replicable Algorithm for
\Pi_{\textit{RandEnc}}

In this subsection, we show that if there exists a replicable polytime algorithm,
\mathcal{B}
, for
\Pi_{\textit{RandEnc}}
, instantiated with a randomizable encryption scheme
\mathcal{E}
, then there exists an adversary breaking the security guarantee of
\mathcal{E}
.
To break security, the adversary must be able to distinguish whether a ciphertext
c
encrypts a 1 or a 0 with probability noticeably better than a coin flip.

The high level idea is as follows. The adversary can first use the ciphertext rerandomization procedure to generate a dataset of ciphertexts encrypting the same bit as
c
. It can then generate a dataset of ciphertexts encrypting 0 by encrypting 0 under the public key and rerandomizing the resulting ciphertext.
The adversary will then invoke
\mathcal{B}
on both datasets, fixing the same randomness for both invocations. If the outputs of both invocations are equal, the adversary will guess that
c
encrypts a 0, and guess
c
encrypts 1 otherwise.

Because rerandomization is perfect and
\mathcal{B}
is a replicable algorithm for
\Pi_{\textit{RandEnc}}
, if
c
encrypts a 0,
\mathcal{B}
will with high probability produce the same output ciphertext for both invocations.
If
c
encrypts a 1,
\mathcal{B}
will can only output the same ciphertext for both invocations if one of the two invocations is incorrect, and so with good probability, the two outputs will differ.
This implies the adversary will have good distinguishing probability, breaking the security of the underlying cryptosystem.

Result:

Outputs a bit
b^{\prime}

Input:
public key
\mathbf{pk}
, ciphertext
c

Algorithm:

1.

Draw a random string
r

2.

c_{0}\leftarrow\mathsf{Enc}(\mathbf{pk},0)

3.

Generate a set
S_{0}
of
m
ciphertexts by running
\mathsf{Rerandomize}(\mathbf{pk},c_{0})

m
times

4.

c_{0}\leftarrow\mathcal{B}(\mathbf{pk},S_{0};r)

5.

Generate a sample
S
of
m
ciphertexts by running
\mathsf{Rerandomize}(\mathbf{pk},c)

m
times

6.

c\leftarrow\mathcal{B}(\mathbf{pk},S;r)

7.

if
c_{0}=c
, then
b^{\prime}=0
, otherwise
b^{\prime}=1

return

b^{\prime}

Algorithm 5

Adversary
\mathcal{A}

Lemma 4.5
.

Let
\mathcal{E}
be a randomizeable encryption scheme, let
\Pi_{\textit{RandEnc}}^{\mathcal{E}}
denote the instantiation of
\Pi_{\textit{RandEnc}}
with
\mathcal{E}
. Let
\mathcal{B}
be a
\rho
-replicable algorithm for
\Pi_{\textit{RandEnc}}^{\mathcal{E}}
with failure probability
\beta
, running in time
\poly(\lambda,\rho,\beta)
, and with sample complexity
m\in\poly(\lambda,\rho,\beta)
. Then there exists an adversary
\mathcal{A}
running in time
\poly(\lambda,\rho,\beta)
such that

\mathbf{Pr}[\mathcal{A}(\mathbf{pk},c)=1\mid c\leftarrow\mathsf{Enc}(\mathbf{pk},1)]-\mathbf{Pr}[\mathcal{A}(\mathbf{pk},c)=1\mid c\leftarrow\mathsf{Enc}(\mathbf{pk},0)]\geq 1-2\beta-\rho.

Proof.

The adversary
\mathcal{A}(\mathbf{pk},c)
outputs 1 whenever
c_{0}\neq c
. The distribution from which
S_{0}
is drawn is supported entirely on encryptions of
0
and, conditioned on
c\leftarrow\mathsf{Enc}(\mathbf{pk},1)
, the distribution from which
S
is drawn is supported entirely on encryptions of
1
.
Then
c_{0}\neq c
except when one of the two calls to
\mathcal{B}
is incorrect, which happens with probability at most
2\beta
. Conditioned on
c\leftarrow\mathsf{Enc}(\mathbf{pk},0)
,
S_{0}
and
S
comprise i.i.d. samples from the same distribution over encryptions of 0.
In this case,
c_{0}\neq c
if either call to
\mathcal{B}
fails to be replicable, which happens with probability at most
\rho
.
Therefore

\mathbf{Pr}[\mathcal{A}(\mathbf{pk},c)=1\mid c\leftarrow\mathsf{Enc}(\mathbf{pk},1)]-\mathbf{Pr}[\mathcal{A}(\mathbf{pk},c)=1\mid c\leftarrow\mathsf{Enc}(\mathbf{pk},0)]\geq 1-2\beta-\rho.

∎

In particular, taking
\beta,\rho
to be constant in Lemma
4.5
gives an adversary breaking the security of
\mathcal{E}
, yielding the following theorem as a corollary.

Theorem 4.6
.

Let
\mathcal{E}
be a randomizeable encryption scheme, and let
\Pi_{\textit{RandEnc}}^{\mathcal{E}}
denote the instantiation of
\Pi_{\textit{RandEnc}}
with
\mathcal{E}
. Then there does not exist a
\rho
-replicable algorithm for
\Pi_{\textit{RandEnc}}^{\mathcal{E}}
with failure probability
\beta
, running in time
\poly(1/\lambda)
, for
\rho<1/4
and
\beta<1/8
.

Proof.

If there exists a
\rho
-replicable algorithm
\mathcal{B}
for
\Pi_{\textit{RandEnc}}^{\mathcal{E}}
with failure probability
\beta<1/8
and replicability parameter
\rho<1/4
running in time
\poly(\lambda)
, then by Lemma
4.5
, there exists an adversary
\mathcal{A}
running in time
\poly(\lambda)
such that

\mathbf{Pr}[\mathcal{A}(\mathbf{pk},c)=1\mid c\leftarrow\mathsf{Enc}(\mathbf{pk},1)]-\mathbf{Pr}[\mathcal{A}(\mathbf{pk},c)=1\mid c\leftarrow\mathsf{Enc}(\mathbf{pk},0)]\geq 1/2>\mathrm{negl}(\lambda),

and therefore
\mathcal{A}
breaks the security of
\mathcal{E}
.
∎

4.1.3
Instantiating
\Pi_{\textit{RandEnc}}
with the Goldwasser-Micali Cryptosystem

Here we recall the high-level structure of the Goldwasser-Micali public-key cryptosystem, introduced in
[
43
]
. The security of the cryptosystem relies on the hardness of deciding quadratic residuosity for integers modulo a semiprime
N
. Informally, encryptions of
0
are quadratic residues modulo
N
, while encryptions of
1
are non-residues. Because multiplying an integer
c
by a quadratic residue modulo
N
preserves quadratic residuosity of
c
, Goldwasser-Micali ciphertexts can be efficiently rerandomized with only a public key. The rerandomization procedure will pick a quadratic residue
r^{2}
uniformly at random, and output its product with the given ciphertext modulo
N
.

Definition 4.7

(Goldwasser-Micali Cryptosystem (
[
43
]
))
.

The Goldwasser-Micali cryptosystem is defined over a plaintext message space
\mathcal{M}=\{0,1\}
and ciphertext space
\mathcal{C}=\mathbb{Z}^{*}_{N}
, for
N
a semiprime. The cryptosystem comprises the following routines.

•

\mathsf{KeyGen}(\lambda)
: Sample
p,q
distinct primes of bit-length
O(\lambda)
and let
N=pq
. Choose
x
to be a quadratic non-residue modulo
N
with Jacobi symbol
\left(\frac{x}{p}\right)=\left(\frac{x}{q}\right)=-1
. Let
\mathbf{sk}=(p,q)
,
\mathbf{pk}=(N,x)
, and output
(\mathbf{sk},\mathbf{pk})
.

•

\mathsf{Enc}(\mathbf{pk},b)
: To encrypt a bit
b\in\{0,1\}
, sample
u\leftarrow_{\mathcal{U}}\mathbb{Z}^{*}_{N}
and output
u^{2}x^{b}\mod N
.

•

\mathsf{Dec}(\mathbf{sk},c)
: To decrypt a ciphertext
c
, output
\bot
if
\gcd(c,N)\neq 1
,
1
if
c
is not a quadratic residue modulo
N
and 0 otherwise.

We now show that the Goldwasser-Micali cryptosystem satisfies the strong rerandomization property described above. We define the verification procedure
\mathcal{V}(\mathbf{pk},c)
to output 1 if
\gcd(c,N)=1
and 0 otherwise. We define
\mathsf{Rerandomize}(\mathbf{pk},c)
to be the procedure that samples
r
uniformly at random from
\mathbb{Z}_{N}^{*}
and outputs
(\mathbf{pk},r^{2}c\mod N)
.

Lemma 4.8
.

The Goldwasser-Micali cryptosystem is a rerandomizeable encryption scheme (
definition

4.2
), for the rerandomization procedure described above.

Proof.

Because
\mathsf{Dec}(\mathbf{sk},c)=\bot
if and only if
\gcd(c,N)\neq 1
, and
\mathcal{V}(\mathbf{pk},c)=0
if and only if
\gcd(c,N)\neq 1
,
\mathcal{V}
satisfies the requirement of
definition

4.2
. The rerandomization procedure multiplies a ciphertext
c
by a random quadratic residue modulo
N
, and therefore preserves quadratic residuosity of
c
. This in turn preserves the plaintext message encrypted by
c
, and so
\mathsf{Rerandomize}
satisfies
\mathsf{Dec}(\mathbf{sk},\mathsf{Rerandomize}(\mathbf{pk},c))=\mathsf{Dec}(\mathbf{sk},c)
.

To show that for all
c,c^{\prime}
such that
\mathsf{Dec}(\mathbf{sk},c)=\mathsf{Dec}(\mathbf{sk},c^{\prime})
,
d_{TV}(\mathsf{Rerandomize}(\mathbf{pk},c),\mathsf{Rerandomize}(\mathbf{pk},c^{\prime}))=0
, let
b\in\{0,1\}
,
c=u^{2}x^{b}\mod N
and
c^{\prime}=v^{2}x^{b}\mod N
be honest encryptions of
b
under
\mathbf{pk}=(N,x)
. It follows that

\displaystyle\mathbf{Pr}[\mathsf{Rerandomize}(\mathbf{pk},c)=a]

\displaystyle=\mathbf{Pr}[\mathsf{Rerandomize}(\mathbf{pk},u^{2}x^{b}\mod N)=a]

\displaystyle=\mathbf{Pr}[r^{2}u^{2}x^{b}=a\mod N]

\displaystyle=\mathbf{Pr}[r^{2}=u^{-2}x^{-b}a\mod N]

\displaystyle=\mathbf{Pr}[(u^{-1}vr)^{2}=u^{-2}x^{-b}a\mod N]

\displaystyle=\mathbf{Pr}[r^{2}v^{2}x^{b}=a\mod N]

\displaystyle=\mathbf{Pr}[\mathsf{Rerandomize}(\mathbf{pk},c^{\prime})=a],

where the fourth equality follows from
r
being chosen uniformly at random from
\mathbb{Z}^{*}_{N}
.
∎

4.2
Correlated Sampling via One-Way Function Inverters

As we saw in
Section

3.3
, correlated sampling gives a generic way for converting a perfectly generalizing algorithm into a replicable one.
In this section, we show that the existence of efficient one-way function inverters implies the ability to efficiently perform correlated sampling on arbitrary distributions over
\{0,1\}^{n}
. Specifically, we show that
if there are no non-uniform
one-way functions, then there is polynomial time implicit correlated sampling.

Theorem 4.9
.

Assuming uniform one-way function inverters exist (Definition
4.11
),
Algorithm
\mathtt{CorrSamp}
is an
(m,n,\nu)
-implicit correlated sampling algorithm that runs in time polynomial in
m
,
n
, and
1/\nu
.

Proof.

The distributional accuracy property is shown in the proof of Lemma
4.14
.
The correlated sampling property is shown in the proof of Lemma
4.16
.
The runtime of
\mathtt{CorrSamp}
is shown in the proof of Lemma
4.17
.
∎

4.2.1
Relevant Definitions

We model samplable distributions by considering the distribution induced by giving random inputs to circuits. Furthermore, we allow for a distributional error parameter
\nu
, giving some slack in the correctness of a correlated sampler.

Definition 4.10

(Implicit Correlated Sampling Algorithm)
.

Let
m,n\in\mathbb{Z}^{+}
, and let
C:\{0,1\}^{m}\rightarrow\{0,1\}^{n}
denote a circuit.
Let distributional error parameter
\nu>0
.

\mathcal{B}(C,\nu;r)
is an
(m,n,\nu)
-implicit correlated sampling algorithm
if the following conditions hold:

1.

Inputs/Outputs:

\mathcal{B}
takes as input a circuit
C:\{0,1\}^{m}\rightarrow\{0,1\}^{n}
, a distributional error parameter
\nu
, and a random string
r
.
\mathcal{B}
outputs a string in
\{0,1\}^{n}
.

2.

\nu
-distributional accuracy:
For all circuits
C:\{0,1\}^{m}\rightarrow\{0,1\}^{n}
, the distributions
D_{C}
and
D_{\mathcal{B}(C,\nu)}
satisfy
d_{\mathrm{T}V}(D_{C},D_{\mathcal{B}(C,\nu)})\leq O(\nu)
.

Here,
D_{C}
denotes the distribution over
\{0,1\}^{n}
induced by querying
C(r)
on uniformly random inputs
r
, i.e., probability density function
p_{D_{C}}(x)=\mathbf{Pr}_{r\sim U^{m}}[C(r)=x]
.
Similarly,

D_{\mathcal{B}(C,\nu)}
denotes the distribution over
\{0,1\}^{n}
induced by querying
\mathcal{B}(C,\nu;r)
with uniformly random strings
r
.

3.

Correlated sampling:
For all pairs of circuits
C_{1},C_{2}:\{0,1\}^{m}\rightarrow\{0,1\}^{n}
,

\mathbf{Pr}_{r}[\mathcal{B}(C_{1},\nu;r)\neq\mathcal{B}(C_{2},\nu;r)]\in O(d_{\mathrm{T}V}(D_{C_{1}},D_{C_{2}})+\nu)
.

We assume we can invert any one-way function on almost all inputs. Specifically, we assume that there is no non-uniform one-way function family, so
that there is a uniform way of inverting any circuit computing a function via a polynomial-time inverter.

Definition 4.11

(Uniform One-Way Function Inverters)
.

Let
\nu^{\prime}>0
.
\mathcal{I}_{\nu^{\prime}}(C,y)
is a
uniform one-way function inverter with error
\nu^{\prime}
if, for any circuit
C:\{0,1\}^{m}\rightarrow\{0,1\}^{n}
,

\mathbf{Pr}_{r^{\prime}\sim\{0,1\}^{m}}[C(\mathcal{I}_{\nu^{\prime}}(C,C(r^{\prime})))=C(r^{\prime})]\geq 1-\nu^{\prime}
, and
\mathcal{I}
runs in randomized polynomial time in
m
,
n
, and
1/\nu^{\prime}
.

In this argument, we will choose
\nu^{\prime}
to be inverse polynomially small in
m,n,
and
1/\nu
.
In addition, we assume that
C(r)
can be efficiently computed.
8
8

8

Note that if there is no such efficient circuit
C
that produces a sample from a distribution
D_{C}
(on a uniformly random input), then
D_{C}
is hard to sample from, and designing an efficient correlated sampling algorithm whose marginal distribution is
D_{C}
(when given circuit
C
) is hopeless.
Thus, we can check if and when the inverter succeeds. For notational convenience, we say that the inverter
\mathcal{I}
returns “
\perp
” if it does not succeed.

Our correlated sampler randomly samples from pairwise-independent hash families in its subroutines.

Definition 4.12

(Pairwise-Independent Hash Family)
.

A family of Boolean functions
\mathcal{H}=\{H|H:\{0,1\}^{m}\rightarrow\{0,1\}^{n}\}
is
pairwise-independent
if, for all
r_{1}\neq r_{2}\in\{0,1\}^{m}
and
x_{1},x_{2}\in\{0,1\}^{n}
,

\mathbf{Pr}_{H\in\mathcal{H}}[H(r_{1})=x_{1}\land H(r_{2})=x_{2}]=2^{-2n}
.

4.2.2
Algorithm Overview

A correlated sampling algorithm
\mathcal{B}
accomplishes two goals. First,
\mathcal{B}(C,\nu;r)
needs to accurately sample from the distribution
D_{C}
. Second,
\mathcal{B}
must convert a random string
r
into the same output when run on distributionally close circuits
C_{1}
and
C_{2}
, with high probability. In other words,
\mathcal{B}
must choose a consistent way to map random strings
r
to elements in the support of
D_{C}
.

For intuition, consider a restricted case of correlated sampling problems in which the distributions
D_{C}
induced by random inputs to circuits
C:\{0,1\}^{m}\rightarrow\{0,1\}^{n}
are promised to be uniformly supported on
2^{\ell}
elements
x\in\{0,1\}^{n}
for some fixed
\ell
. Let
k
be a small slack parameter, and consider the following sampler:

1.

Draw a random hash function
H:\{0,1\}^{n}\to\{0,1\}^{\ell+k}

2.

Draw a random string
u\in\{0,1\}^{\ell+k}

3.

Run the inverter on
u
:
r=\mathcal{I}_{\nu^{\prime}}(C\circ H,u)

4.

If
r=\bot
(i.e.
u
is not in the support of
C\circ H
), repeat. Else return
y^{*}=C(r)

Randomness
\{0,1\}^{m}

High-level structure of correlated sampler for uniform

The high level idea is that
k
can be chosen large enough such that
\mathtt{supp}(D_{C})
has few collisions with good probability (for random
H
), but small enough s.t.
2^{k}
(and therefore the runtime) remains polynomial in the relevant parameters. Assuming no collisions occur, it is easy to see this process is a correlated sampler since each element in the support of
D_{C}
is sampled uniformly at random, and moreover applied to distinct circuits
C_{1}
and
C_{2}
,
y^{*}
only differs if the sampler hits a hash value
u
that contains an element in the symmetric difference
\mathtt{supp}(C_{1})\Delta\mathtt{supp}(C_{2})
. Since there are no collisions, this occurs exactly with probability
d_{TV}(D_{C_{1}},D_{C_{2}})
as desired.

Moving to the general case, our algorithm
\mathtt{CorrSamp}
applies this idea as follows.
\mathtt{CorrSamp}
divides the distribution
D_{C}
into “levels”
\ell
, such that each level contains elements in the support with probability density near
2^{-\ell}
(specifically, those in the range
(2^{-\ell-1},2^{-\ell}]
). We now pick a level uniformly at random, and hope to apply the above process. Intuitively, the main challenge is that given the output
y^{*}
, we need to ensure
y^{*}
actually belongs at level
\ell
. This is done through the introduction of a
second hash function

H_{2}:\{0,1\}^{m}\to\{0,1\}^{m-\ell+k}
. In particular, fixing
u
and
y^{*}
as in the simplified variant, we wish to estimate
|C^{-1}(y^{*})|=|(H\circ C)^{-1}(u)|
(assuming no collisions). To do this, we call the inverter on the concatenated function
F_{C,l,H,H_{2}}(r)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle{\mathrm{\scriptstyle def}}}}{{=}}H(C(r))\mathbin{\|}H_{2}(r)
9
9

9

We use ‘
\mathbin{\|}
’ to denote concatenation of strings.
on many pairs of the form
(u||v)
, where
v\in\{0,1\}^{m-\ell+k}
is chosen uniformly at random. Since we have fixed
u
, the inverter can only succeed on this call when
v=H_{2}(r)
for some
r\in(C\circ H)^{-1}(u)
. Since
v
is chosen uniformly at random, the success probability of the inverter is then directly proportional to the density of
y^{*}
, allowing us to determine whether or not
y^{*}
is in level
\ell
with high probability.
10
10

10

Of course this only holds assuming few collisions. To handle the general case, we actually draw a new
H_{2}
with every choice
v
to ensure this holds across all rounds.
We can then return
y^{*}
if it is in the chosen level, and repeat the process from the beginning if not. Because we have chosen
u
uniformly at random from
\{0,1\}^{\ell+k}
, for any
x
it holds that
x=H^{-1}(u)
with probability
2^{-\ell-k}
(assuming no collisions). Then this approach allows us to sample uniformly from level
\ell
, where every
x
in level
\ell
is output with probability proportional to
2^{-\ell}
. Note that since the true density may be a constant factor away from
2^{-\ell}
, this is not yet quite enough to achieve our true target distributional accuracy—we will address this detail in the next section.

\ell=1\mid x:p_{D}(x)\in(1/4,1/2]
\vdots

4.2.3
Algorithm Description and Pseudocode

We now give pseudocode for the
\mathtt{CorrSamp}
algorithm and its subroutines, in addition to a more detailed description. The main algorithm
\mathtt{CorrSamp}
takes as input a circuit
C
and an error parameter
\nu
. As described in Section
4.2.2
, at each iteration of the main loop,
\mathtt{CorrSamp}
picks a level
\ell
uniformly at random, and then tries to sample an element that has probability roughly
2^{-\ell}
under
D_{C}
. In addition to drawing a hash function
H_{1}
with range
\{0,1\}^{\ell+k}
, and an element
u
from that range, it will also sample a random threshold parameter
\beta\in(1,2]
, and invoke the subroutine
\mathtt{ElemFind}_{C,\nu,\ell,\beta}(H_{1},u)
with these parameters and inputs. We will properly motivate this new parameter
\beta
shortly, but looking ahead, it will help us avoid the distributional accuracy issues present in Section
4.2.2
.

In the pseudocode for
\mathtt{CorrSamp}
(Algorithm
6
),
k
is chosen to be large enough that we will be able to avoid problematic collisions for all hash functions with high probability, but small enough to ensure polynomial runtime. The value
T_{1}
is chosen to be large enough that
\mathtt{CorrSamp}
returns
x\neq\bot
with high probability, but also small enough such that we can guarantee certain simplifying assumptions will hold with high probability across all rounds of
\mathtt{CorrSamp}
.

k\leftarrow\Theta(\log(m)+\log(1/\nu))

T_{1}\leftarrow\Theta(mk2^{k}\log(1/\nu))

for

t=1
to
t=T_{1}

do

1

\beta\leftarrow_{r}[1,2]

2

\ell\leftarrow_{r}\{0,1,\dots,m\}

3

H_{1}\leftarrow_{r}
pairwise independent hash function from
n
bits to
\ell+k
bits

4

u\leftarrow_{r}\{0,1\}^{\ell+k}

5

x\leftarrow\mathtt{ElemFind}_{C,\nu,\ell,\beta}(H_{1},u;r)

6

x\neq\bot

then

return

x

end

if

end

for

return

Algorithm 6

\mathtt{CorrSamp}(C,\nu;r)

Input: Circuit
C:\{0,1\}^{m}\rightarrow\{0,1\}^{n}
, distributional error parameter
\nu
, and random string
r

Output: An element
x\in\{0,1\}^{n}

The subroutine
\mathtt{ElemFind}_{C,\nu,\ell,\beta}(H_{1},u;r)
follows the approach described in Section
4.2.2
, estimating the probability of
y^{*}=H_{1}^{-1}(u)
under
D_{C}
by drawing many pairs
(H_{2},v)
of hash functions with elements from their range, and invoking the subroutine
\mathtt{HashCheck}_{C,\nu,\ell,H_{1},u}(H_{2},v)
to invert
H(C(r))||H_{2}(r)
on each
(u,v)
. This procedure approximates the density of random strings
r
mapped to
y^{*}
by
C
. If
y^{*}
is in or nearly in level
\ell
, then
\mathtt{ElemFind}
will obtain an estimate
\widehat{q}(y^{*})
that is close to
p_{D}(y^{*})2^{\ell-k}
. It will then return
y^{*}
only if
\frac{\beta}{2}2^{-k}<\widehat{q}(y^{*})\leq\beta 2^{-k}
, i.e. roughly when
\beta 2^{-\ell-1}<p_{D}(y^{*})\leq\beta 2^{-\ell}
.

We now reach the core reason for choosing our threshold
\beta
randomly. As in Section
4.2.2
, if we did fix
\beta
, then any
y^{*}
at level
\ell
will be sampled whenever
y^{*}\in H_{1}^{-1}(u)
, which happens with probability proportional to
2^{-\ell}
. Since this occurs for any
p_{D}(y^{*})\in[\beta 2^{-\ell-1},\beta 2^{-\ell}]
, this is not a good enough estimate. The key is to observe that choosing
\beta
randomly allows us to avoid this kind of uniform sampling over any particular level. Instead we sample from “fuzzy” levels, where the choice of
\beta
shifts the boundaries while maintaining that the fuzzy levels partition
[0,1]
.
In slightly more detail, observe that for any
y^{*}
there is some
j
such that

p_{D}(y^{*})=\alpha 2^{-j-1}+(1-\alpha)2^{-j}
for
\alpha\in[0,1]
. This means we want
y^{*}
to belong to ‘level’
(j+1)
with probability
\alpha
, and to level
j
with probability
(1-\alpha)
. We will show in Lemma
4.14
that choosing
\beta
uniformly at random exactly achieves this.

In the pseudocode for
\mathtt{ElemFind}
(Algorithm
7
),
k
is chosen to balance the same constraints we have described for
\mathtt{CorrSamp}
. The value for
T_{2}
, the number of hash function and element pairs
(H_{2},v)
used to estimate the probability
p_{D}(y^{*})
, is large enough to ensure a good empirical estimate for
y^{*}
, but small enough to ensure
\mathtt{ElemFind}
still runs in time polynomial in
m,n,
and
1/\nu
.

k\leftarrow\Theta(\log(m)+\log(1/\nu))

T_{2}\leftarrow\Theta(\nu^{-2}\log(1/\nu)T_{1}^{-1}\log(T_{1}^{-1}))

1

for

i=1
to
i=T_{2}

do

2

H^{i}_{2}\leftarrow_{r}
pairwise independent hash function from
m
bits to
m-\ell+k
bits

3

v^{i}\leftarrow_{r}\{0,1\}^{m-\ell+k}

4

\mathtt{HashCheck}_{C,\nu,\ell,H_{1},u}(H_{2}^{i},v^{i};r)

end

5

\widehat{q}_{x}

x
was returned by
\mathtt{HashCheck}

6

\exists

\perp

4.2.4
Analysis – Structure and Simplifying Assumptions

In this section, we analyze the
\mathtt{CorrSamp}
algorithm. Before proceeding with the analysis, we first introduce several simplifying assumptions below we will use throughout. In Section
4.2.5
, we analyze the distributional accuracy of
\mathtt{CorrSamp}
, showing that its distribution over outputs is close to the target distribution. In Section
4.2.6
, we analyze the success probability of
\mathtt{CorrSamp}
as a correlated sampler, showing that for two circuits
C_{1}
and
C_{2}
,
\mathtt{CorrSamp}(C_{1},\nu;r)=\mathtt{CorrSamp}(C_{2},\nu;r)
except with probability proportional to
d_{\mathrm{T}V}(D_{C_{1}},D_{C_{2}})
. In Section
4.2.7
, we show that
\mathtt{CorrSamp}
runs in time polynomial in the
m
,
n
, and
1/\nu
. Finally, in Section
4.2.8
we show that our simplifying assumptions hold for the entire execution of
\mathtt{CorrSamp}
, except with high probability. Thus, all statements about the behavior of
\mathtt{CorrSamp}
under the ideal conditions will still hold without these assumptions, except with probability
O(\nu)
.

Definition 4.13

(Ideal Conditions)
.

We collectively refer to the following as the
ideal conditions
.

•

The inverter
\mathcal{I}_{\nu^{\prime}}
never fails: for all
x
and
r
on which
\mathtt{CorrSamp}
invokes
\mathcal{I}_{\nu^{\prime}}(x;r)
,
\mathcal{I}_{\nu^{\prime}}(x;r)\neq\bot
.

•

For every
\ell
, there is at most one
y^{*}\in H_{1}^{-1}(u)
s.t.

p_{D}(y^{*})\in[2^{-\ell-4},2^{-\ell+4}]

•

\mathtt{ElemFind}
always returns
y^{*}
or ‘
\bot
’. Furthermore, for
y^{*}
returned by
\mathtt{ElemFind}
, we have the stronger condition that
p_{D}(y^{*})\in[2^{-\ell-2},2^{-\ell+2}].

•

The empirical estimate of
\widehat{q}(y^{*})
is good:

\hat{q}(y^{*})\in\left(1\pm O\left(\nu\right)\right)p_{D}(y^{*})2^{\ell-k}

4.2.5
Analysis – Distributional Accuracy.

In this section, we analyze the distributional accuracy of
\mathtt{CorrSamp}
. Denote by
D_{\mathtt{CorrSamp}}
the distribution over outputs of
\mathtt{CorrSamp}(C,\nu)
. We show that
d_{\mathrm{T}V}(D_{\mathtt{CorrSamp}},D_{C})\leq O(\nu)
, assuming the ideal conditions of Definition
4.13
.

Lemma 4.14

(Distributional Accuracy of
\mathtt{CorrSamp}
)
.

For all circuits
C:\{0,1\}^{m}\rightarrow\{0,1\}^{n}
, the distributions
D_{C}
and
D_{\mathtt{CorrSamp}}
satisfy
d_{\mathrm{T}V}(D_{C},D_{\mathtt{CorrSamp}})\leq O(\nu)
, assuming the ideal conditions of Definition
4.13
hold for all rounds of
\mathtt{CorrSamp}
.
Here,
D_{C}
and
D_{\mathtt{CorrSamp}}
denote the distributions over
\{0,1\}^{n}
induced by querying
C(r)
and
\mathtt{CorrSamp}(C,\nu;r)
respectively with uniformly random strings
r
.

We first prove the following useful lemma, bounding the probability that any
x\in\mathtt{supp}(D_{C})
is returned in a single round.

Lemma 4.15
.

Fix an
x\in\mathtt{supp}(D_{C})
. Then

\mathbf{Pr}_{H_{1},u,\ell,\beta}[\mathtt{ElemFind}_{C,\nu,\ell,\beta}(H_{1},u)=x]\in\frac{p_{D}(x)(1+O(\nu))}{(m+1)2^{k}}
.

Proof.

For any
\ell
, we have that
\mathbf{Pr}_{H_{1},u}[x=H_{1}^{-1}(u)]=2^{-\ell-k}
. Conditioned on
x
being selected by
H_{1}
and
u
, by construction,
\mathtt{ElemFind}_{C,\nu,\ell,\beta}
returns
x
whenever
\widehat{q}(x)\in(\frac{\beta}{2}2^{-k},\beta 2^{-k}]
.
Rewriting
p_{D}(x)=\gamma 2^{-j}
for
\gamma\in[1/2,1]
, we observe that
x
will only ever have non-zero probability of being returned by
\mathtt{ElemFind}_{C,\nu,\ell,\beta}(H_{1},u)
when
j-1\leq\ell\leq j+2
, from the assumptions of Definition
4.13
. Since we have
\widehat{q}(x)\in(1\pm O(\nu))p_{D}(x)2^{\ell-k}
for any
\ell
in this range, it follows that for these
\ell
,

\displaystyle\mathbf{Pr}_{\beta}\left[\widehat{q}(x)\in(\tfrac{\beta}{2}2^{-k},\beta 2^{-k}]\right]

\displaystyle\in\mathbf{Pr}_{\beta}[\tfrac{\beta}{2}<p_{D}(x)2^{\ell}(1\pm O(\nu))\leq\beta]

\displaystyle\in\mathbf{Pr}_{\beta}[\tfrac{\beta}{2}<p_{D}(x)2^{\ell}\leq\beta]\pm O(\nu)p(x)2^{\ell}.

Recalling that
\ell
is chosen uniformly at random from
\{0,\dots,m\}
, we can then write the probability that
\mathtt{ElemFind}
returns
x
as

\displaystyle\mathbf{Pr}_{H_{1},u,\ell,\beta}[\mathtt{ElemFind}_{C,\nu,\ell,\beta}(H_{1},u)=x]

\displaystyle\in\sum_{\ell\in[j-1,j+2]}\frac{\mathbf{Pr}_{\beta}[\tfrac{\beta}{2}<p_{D}(x)2^{\ell}\leq\beta]\pm O(\nu)p_{D}(x)2^{\ell}}{(m+1)2^{\ell+k}}

\displaystyle\in\left(\sum_{\ell\in[j-1,j+2]}\frac{\mathbf{Pr}_{\beta}[\tfrac{\beta}{2}<p_{D}(x)2^{\ell}\leq\beta]}{(m+1)2^{\ell+k}}\right)\pm\frac{O(\nu)p_{D}(x)}{(m+1)2^{k}}.

Observing that
\mathbf{Pr}_{\beta}[\tfrac{\beta}{2}<p_{D}(x)2^{\ell}\leq\beta]=0
except for
\ell\in\{j,j+1\}
, we can simplify the series:

\displaystyle\sum_{\ell\in[j-1,j+2]}\frac{\mathbf{Pr}_{\beta}[\tfrac{\beta}{2}<p_{D}(x)2^{\ell}\leq\beta]}{(m+1)2^{\ell+k}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{\ell\in[j,j+1]}\frac{\mathbf{Pr}_{\beta}[\tfrac{\beta}{2}<p_{D}(x)2^{\ell}\leq\beta]}{(m+1)2^{\ell+k}}

\displaystyle=\frac{\mathbf{Pr}_{\beta}[\tfrac{\beta}{2}<p_{D}(x)2^{j}\leq\beta]}{(m+1)2^{j+k}}+\frac{\mathbf{Pr}_{\beta}[\tfrac{\beta}{2}<p_{D}(x)2^{j+1}\leq\beta]}{(m+1)2^{j+k+1}}

\displaystyle=\frac{\mathbf{Pr}_{\beta}[\tfrac{\beta}{2}<\gamma\leq\beta]}{(m+1)2^{j+k}}+\frac{\mathbf{Pr}_{\beta}[\tfrac{\beta}{2}<2\gamma\leq\beta]}{(m+1)2^{j+k+1}}

\displaystyle=\frac{\mathbf{Pr}_{\beta}[\beta<2\gamma]}{(m+1)2^{j+k}}+\frac{\mathbf{Pr}_{\beta}[\beta\geq 2\gamma]}{(m+1)2^{j+k+1}}

\displaystyle=\frac{2\gamma-1}{(m+1)2^{j+k}}+\frac{2-2\gamma}{(m+1)2^{j+k+1}}

\displaystyle=\frac{2\gamma-1+1-\gamma}{(m+1)2^{j+k}}

\displaystyle=\frac{p_{D}(x)}{(m+1)2^{k}}.

Plugging back into the probability that
\mathtt{ElemFind}
returns
x
, we have

\displaystyle\mathbf{Pr}_{H_{1},u,\ell,\beta}[\mathtt{ElemFind}_{C,\nu,\ell,\beta}(H_{1},u)=x]

\displaystyle\in\frac{p_{D}(x)(1+O(\nu))}{(m+1)2^{k}}.

∎

Finally, we can show that the output distribution of
\mathtt{CorrSamp}
and that of circuit
C
are
O(\nu)
-close in variation distance.

Proof.

Proof of Lemma
4.14
:

From Lemma
4.15
, we have that in each round,
x
is returned by
\mathtt{ElemFind}
with probability

\frac{p_{D}(x)(1\pm O(\nu))}{(m+1)2^{k}}
.

Summing over all
x
, the probability that
\mathtt{CorrSamp}
terminates in any individual round is in the range

\frac{1\pm O(\nu)}{(m+1)2^{k}}
.
So, conditioned on a round of
\mathtt{CorrSamp}
returning, the algorithm returns
x
with probability in
(1\pm O(\nu))p_{D}(x)
. Finally,
\mathtt{CorrSamp}
does not return by the final
T_{1}
’th round with probability at most
O(\nu)
, by the choice of
T_{1}
. Altogether, this implies
d_{\mathrm{T}V}(D_{C},D_{\mathtt{CorrSamp}})\in O(\nu)
as desired.
∎

4.2.6
Analysis — Correlated Sampling.

Next, we show that
\mathtt{CorrSamp}
satisfies the correlated sampling requirement of Definition
4.10
. To simplify notation in this subsection, we will denote the probability of an element
x
under
D_{C_{1}}
and
D_{C_{2}}
by
p_{1}(x)
and
p_{2}(x)
respectively.

Lemma 4.16

(Correlated Sampling of
\mathtt{CorrSamp}
)
.

For all pairs of circuits
C_{1},C_{2}:\{0,1\}^{m}\rightarrow\{0,1\}^{n}
, assuming the ideal conditions hold for all rounds of
\mathtt{CorrSamp}(C_{1},\nu;r)
and
\mathtt{CorrSamp}(C_{2},\nu;r)
,

\mathbf{Pr}_{r}[\mathtt{CorrSamp}(C_{1},\nu;r)\neq\mathtt{CorrSamp}(C_{2},\nu;r)]\leq O(d_{\mathrm{T}V}(D_{C_{1}},D_{C_{2}})+\nu).

Proof.

Let
E_{0}
denote the event that
\mathtt{CorrSamp}(C_{1},\nu;r)\neq\mathtt{CorrSamp}(C_{2},\nu;r)
, and for simplicity of notation, shorten
\mathtt{ElemFind}
to
\mathtt{EF}
, and write
\mathtt{EF}^{(i)}_{C_{j}}
to denote the output of
\mathtt{ElemFind}
in the
i
th round. We start by making some simplifying assumptions. First, observe that since the probability
\mathtt{CorrSamp}
returns
\bot
is always at most
O(\nu)
, we can condition on the fact that both
\mathtt{CorrSamp}(C_{2},\nu;r)\neq\bot
and
\mathtt{CorrSamp}(C_{1},\nu;r)\neq\bot
without loss of generality. Second, we can assume by symmetry that
\mathtt{CorrSamp}(C_{1},\nu;r)
does not return before
\mathtt{CorrSamp}(C_{2},\nu;r)
(else relabel
C_{1}
as
C_{2}
).

With this in mind, let
E_{i}
denote the event that
\mathtt{CorrSamp}(C_{2},\nu;r)
returns in round
i
. The event
E_{0}
can then be bounded by

\mathbf{Pr}[E_{0}]\leq\sum\limits_{i}\mathbf{Pr}[E_{i}]\mathbf{Pr}_{\beta,\ell}[\mathtt{EF}^{(i)}_{C_{1}}\neq\mathtt{EF}^{(i)}_{C_{2}}\mid E_{i}].

To bound the probability of this inner event, observe that under our assumptions, this occurs exactly when
x=\mathtt{EF}^{(i)}_{C_{2}}
, but for the empirical estimate computed by
\mathtt{EF}^{(i)}_{C_{1}}
,
\widehat{q}_{1}(x)\not\in[\frac{\beta}{2}2^{-k},\beta 2^{-k}]
. We will show conditioned on any value of
x
and
\ell
, this occurs with probability at most:

\mathbf{Pr}_{\beta}[\mathtt{EF}^{(i)}_{C_{1}}\neq x\mid E_{i},\mathtt{EF}^{(i)}_{C_{2}}=x,\ell]\leq O(2^{\ell}|p_{2}(x)-p_{1}(x)|+\nu).

(9)

In this case, we can bound
E_{0}
by conditioning further on
x
and
\ell
as:

\displaystyle\mathbf{Pr}_{r}[E_{0}]

\displaystyle\leq\sum\limits_{i\in[T_{1}]}\mathbf{Pr}_{\beta,\ell}[E_{i}]\mathbf{Pr}_{\beta,\ell}[\mathtt{EF}^{(i)}_{C_{1}}\neq\mathtt{EF}^{(i)}_{C_{2}}\mid E_{i}]

\displaystyle\leq\sum\limits_{i\in[T_{1}]}\mathbf{Pr}_{\beta,\ell}[E_{i}]\sum_{x\neq\bot}\mathbf{Pr}_{\beta,\ell}[\mathtt{EF}^{(i)}_{C_{1}}\neq\mathtt{EF}^{(i)}_{C_{2}}\mid E_{i},\mathtt{EF}^{(i)}_{C_{2}}=x]\mathbf{Pr}_{\beta,\ell}[\mathtt{EF}^{(i)}_{C_{2}}=x|E_{i}]

\displaystyle\leq O(\nu)+\sum\limits_{i\in[T_{1}]}\mathbf{Pr}_{\beta,\ell}[E_{i}]\sum_{x\neq\bot}\mathbf{Pr}_{\beta,\ell}[\mathtt{EF}^{(i)}_{C_{1}}\neq\mathtt{EF}^{(i)}_{C_{2}}\mid E_{i},\mathtt{EF}^{(i)}_{C_{2}}=x]p_{2}(x)

\displaystyle\leq O(\nu)+\sum\limits_{i\in[T_{1}]}\mathbf{Pr}_{\beta,\ell}[E_{i}]\sum_{x\neq\bot}p_{2}(x)\sum\limits_{\ell}\mathbf{Pr}[\ell\mid E_{i},\mathtt{EF}^{(i)}_{C_{2}}=x]\mathbf{Pr}_{\beta}[\mathtt{EF}^{(i)}_{C_{1}}\neq\mathtt{EF}^{(i)}_{C_{2}}\mid E_{i},\mathtt{EF}^{(i)}_{C_{2}}=x,\ell]

\displaystyle\leq O(\nu)+\sum\limits_{i\in[T_{1}]}\mathbf{Pr}_{\beta,\ell}[E_{i}]\sum_{x\neq\bot}p_{2}(x)\sum\limits_{\ell}\mathbf{Pr}[\ell\mid E_{i},\mathtt{EF}^{(i)}_{C_{2}}=x]O(2^{\ell}|p_{2}(x)-p_{1}(x)|).

Under the ideal conditions, the posterior of
\ell
is
0
unless
p_{2}(x)\in[2^{-\ell-2},2^{-\ell+2}]
, so altogether:

\displaystyle\mathbf{Pr}_{r}[E_{0}]

\displaystyle\leq O(\nu)+\sum\limits_{i\in[T_{1}]}\mathbf{Pr}_{\beta,\ell}[E_{i}]\sum_{x\neq\bot}p_{2}(x)\sum\limits_{\ell}\mathbf{Pr}[\ell\mid E_{i},\mathtt{EF}^{(i)}_{C_{2}}=x]O\left(\frac{1}{p_{2}(x)}|p_{2}(x)-p_{1}(x)|\right)

\displaystyle\leq O(\nu)+\sum\limits_{i\in[T_{1}]}\mathbf{Pr}_{\beta,\ell}[E_{i}]\sum_{x\neq\bot}O(|p_{2}(x)-p_{1}(x)|)

\displaystyle\leq O(d_{TV}(D_{C_{1}},D_{C_{2}})+\nu)

as desired.

It is therefore left to prove
Equation

9
, which we analyze the probability by splitting into two cases based on
p_{1}(x)
:

1.

p_{2}(x)/4\leq p_{1}(x)\leq 4p_{2}(x)

2.

p_{1}(x)<p_{2}(x)/4
or
p_{1}(x)>4p_{2}(x)

Case 1:

In this case, because we are assuming the ideal conditions of Definition
4.13
and have conditioned on
\mathtt{ElemFind}_{C_{2},\nu,\ell,\beta}(H_{1},u;r)=x
for some
x
in this round, it must be the case that for this
x
we have:

•

p_{2}(x)\in[2^{-\ell-2},2^{-\ell+2}]

•

p_{1}(x)\in[2^{-\ell-4},2^{-\ell+4}]
(from our assumption that
p_{2}(x)/4\leq p_{1}(x)\leq 4p_{2}(x)
)

•

\widehat{q}_{2}(x)\in(1\pm O(\nu))p_{2}(x)2^{\ell-k}

•

\widehat{q}_{1}(x)\in(1\pm O(\nu))p_{1}(x)2^{\ell-k}
.

From the uniqueness of
x
satisfying
x=H_{1}^{-1}(u)
and
p_{1}(x)\in[2^{\ell-4},2^{-\ell+4}]
, we can assume that
\mathtt{ElemFind}_{C_{1},\nu,\ell,\beta}(H_{1},u;r)
outputs either
x
or
\bot
in this round. Therefore, we can bound the probability
\mathtt{ElemFind}_{C_{1},\nu,\ell,\beta}(H_{1},u;r)\neq\mathtt{ElemFind}_{C_{2},\nu,\ell,\beta}(H_{1},u;r)
by the probability that
\mathtt{ElemFind}_{C_{1},\nu,\ell,\beta}(H_{1},u;r)
outputs
\bot
conditioned on
\mathtt{ElemFind}_{C_{2},\nu,\ell,\beta}(H_{1},u;r)
returning
x
. This occurs whenever
\beta
is chosen so that either

(1+O(\nu))p_{1}(x)2^{\ell-k}<\frac{\beta 2^{-k}}{2}\leq p_{2}(x)2^{\ell-k}(1+O(\nu))

or

p_{2}(x)2^{\ell-k}(1-O(\nu))\leq\beta 2^{-k}<p_{1}(x)2^{\ell-k}(1+O(\nu)).

Observing that these cases are mutually exclusive and the first interval is the largest, we consider only that worst case and rearrange to obtain the condition

\displaystyle\beta

\displaystyle\leq 2^{\ell+1}|p_{2}(x)-p_{1}(x)|+O(\nu)(p_{2}(x)+p_{1}(x))2^{\ell}

\displaystyle\leq 2^{\ell+1}|p_{2}(x)-p_{1}(x)|+O(\nu),

where the last inequality follows from our previously stated bounds
p_{1},p_{2}\in[2^{-\ell-4},2^{-\ell+4}]
.

Since
\beta
is chosen uniformly at random from the interval
[1,2]
, it follows that
\beta
satisfies the condition above with probability no greater than
2^{\ell+1}|p_{2}(x)-p_{1}(x)|+O(\nu)
. Conditioning on
\mathtt{ElemFind}_{C_{2},\nu,\ell,\beta}(H_{1},u;r)=x
and
p_{2}(x)\in[2^{-\ell-4},2^{-\ell+4}]
, we have

\displaystyle\mathbf{Pr}_{\beta}[\mathtt{ElemFind}_{C_{1},\nu,\ell\,\beta}(H_{1},u;r)=\bot]

\displaystyle\leq\mathbf{Pr}_{\beta}[\beta\leq 2^{\ell+1}|p_{2}(x_{2})-p_{1}(x_{2})|+O(\nu)]

\displaystyle\leq 2^{\ell+1}|p_{2}(x)-p_{1}(x)|+O(\nu).

Case 2:

In this case, we have either
p_{1}(x)<p_{2}(x)/4
or
p_{1}(x)>4p_{2}(x)
, and so
p_{2}(x)\in O(|p_{2}(x)-p_{1}(x)|)
. Conditioning on
\mathtt{ElemFind}_{C_{2},\nu,\ell,\beta}(H_{1},u;r)=x
in this round also gives us that
p_{2}(x)=\Theta(2^{-\ell})
, so

\displaystyle\mathbf{Pr}_{\beta}[\mathtt{ElemFind}_{C_{1},\nu,\ell,\beta}(H_{1},u;r)\neq x\mid\mathtt{ElemFind}_{C_{2},\nu,\ell,\beta}(H_{1},u;r)=x]

\displaystyle\leq 1

\displaystyle\leq O(2^{\ell}p_{2}(x_{2}))

\displaystyle\leq O(2^{\ell}|p_{2}(x_{2})-p_{1}(x_{2})|).

Then for any value of
p_{1}(x)
(either in Case 1 or Case 2) we have that

\mathbf{Pr}_{\beta}[\mathtt{ElemFind}_{C_{1},\nu,\ell,\beta}(H_{1},u;r)\neq x\mid\mathtt{ElemFind}_{C_{2},\nu,\ell,\beta}(H_{1},u;r)=x]\in O(2^{\ell}|p_{2}(x)-p_{1}(x)|+\nu)

as desired.
∎

4.2.7
Analysis — Runtime

Lemma 4.17

(Runtime of
\mathtt{CorrSamp}
)
.

Assuming inverter
\mathcal{I}
runs in time polynomial in
m
,
n
, and
1/\nu
, algorithm
\mathtt{CorrSamp}
also runs in time polynomial in
m
,
n
, and
1/\nu
.

Proof.

Algorithm
\mathtt{CorrSamp}
runs for at most
O(mk2^{k}\log(1/\nu))
rounds. Parameter
k
is chosen in
\Theta(\log(m)+\log(1/\nu))
, so
2^{k}\in O(\poly(m,1/\nu))
. Randomly sampling a pairwise-independent hash function from
n
bits to
\ell+k
bits can be done in
\poly(m,n,1/\nu)
time.

Each round of
\mathtt{CorrSamp}
contains a call to
\mathtt{ElemFind}
. In
\mathtt{ElemFind}
, there are
O(\poly(m,1/\nu))
rounds in which a hash function from
m
bits to
m-\ell+k
bits is randomly sampled (
O(\poly(m,1/\nu))
time). Furthermore, each round contains a call to
\mathtt{HashCheck}
, which runs in time
\poly(m,n,1/\nu)
by the assumption that inverter
\mathcal{I}_{\nu^{\prime}}
does as well.

Multiplying these nesting terms together,
\mathtt{CorrSamp}
runs in time polynomial in
m
,
n
, and
\nu
.

Note that the randomness management, which ensures that the same bits of the random string
r
are always used across multiple executions of
\mathtt{CorrSamp}
, can also be done in time polynomial in
m
,
n
, and
1/\nu
. Each algorithm and subroutine has a finite number of randomness calls, and each call can be made using
\poly(m,n,1/\nu)
random bits. Thus,
r
can efficiently be canonically proportioned for all uses of randomness in the algorithm. For more details, see Appendix
C.1
.

Parameter
\beta
is chosen uniformly randomly in
[1,2]
in each loop of
\mathtt{CorrSamp}
. Only polynomial in
m
,
n
, and
1/\nu
bits of precision are needed to choose
\beta
so that the errors introduced by not using uniformly random values are altogether small relative to distributional error parameter
\nu
.
∎

4.2.8
Analysis – Removing Assumption of Ideal Conditions

Proposition 4.18
.

The ideal conditions of Definition
4.13
hold across all steps of
\mathtt{CorrSamp}
with probability at least
1-O(\nu)
.

We break the proof into its four constituent part.

Lemma 4.19

(Inverter Never Fails)
.

Let
\mathcal{S}
denote the set of strings
(x;r)
on which
\mathtt{CorrSamp}
invokes
\mathcal{I}_{\nu^{\prime}}(x;r)
. The probability the inverter fails on
\mathcal{S}
is negligible:

\mathbf{Pr}[\exists(x;r)\in\mathcal{S}:\mathcal{I}_{\nu^{\prime}}(x;r)=\bot]\leq O(\nu),

as long as
\nu^{\prime}=\poly(\nu^{-1},m,n)
is sufficiently small.

Proof.

Recall our inverter has the following guarantee

\mathbf{Pr}_{r^{\prime}\sim\{0,1\}^{m}}[C(\mathcal{I}_{\nu^{\prime}}(C,C(r^{\prime}));r)=C(r^{\prime})]\geq 1-\nu^{\prime},

where
r
stands for the internal randomness of
\mathcal{I}
and
\nu^{\prime}
can be taken to be polynomially small in
m,n
and
\nu
. It will be enough to take the failure rate
\nu^{\prime}\leq O(\frac{\nu^{2}}{T_{1}T_{2}})=\poly(\nu^{-1},m,n)
. We will bound the probability such an inverter fails on a random input
(u||v)
.

First, observe that by Markov’s inequality, most choices of internal randomness
r
for the inverter random work for almost all
r^{\prime}\in\{0,1\}^{m}
:

\mathbf{Pr}_{r}\left[\mathbf{Pr}_{r^{\prime}}\left[\mathcal{I}_{\nu^{\prime}}(C,C(r^{\prime}));r)\neq C(r^{\prime})\right]\geq O\left(\frac{\nu}{T_{1}T_{2}}\right)\right]\leq O(\nu).

Assume then this event does not occur. Our algorithm only fails if the random choice of
(u||v)
is hashed to by a string for which
\mathcal{I}_{\nu^{\prime}}(C,C(r^{\prime}));r)\neq C(r^{\prime})
. In the worst case, these bad strings each correspond to unique
(u||v)\in\{0,1\}^{m+2k}
, in which case we have a total of
\frac{\nu}{T_{1}T_{2}}2^{m}
out of
2^{m+2k}
bad inputs. Union bounding over the
T_{1}T_{2}
applications of the inverter in
\mathtt{CorrSamp}
gives a total failure probability of
1-O(\nu)
as desired.
∎

To prove the remaining conditions, it will be useful first to bound the number of collisions experienced by our hash functions.

Claim 4.20

(Collision Avoidance)
.

With probability at least
1-O(\nu)
, for all choices of
H_{1}
,
u
, and
\ell
in
\mathtt{CorrSamp}
:

1.

H_{1}
has no relevant collisions:

\forall p_{D}(x),p_{D}(x^{\prime})\in[2^{-\ell-4},2^{-\ell+4}]:H_{1}(x)\neq H_{1}(x^{\prime}),

2.

For all
x\in H_{1}^{-1}(u)
, the total number of collisions across choices of
H_{2}
is at most:

\left|\{H_{2},(r,r^{\prime})\in C^{-1}(x):H_{2}(r)=H_{2}(r^{\prime})\}\right|\leq\delta_{x}T_{2}|C^{-1}(x)|,

where
\delta_{x}=O\left(2^{\ell}p_{D}(x)\cdot\frac{mk\log(1/\nu)}{\nu}\cdot 2^{-k}\right)

Proof.

To prove the first condition, observe there are at most
2^{\ell+4}
elements
x\in\{0,1\}^{n}
with measure in the range
p_{D}(x)\in[2^{-\ell-4},2^{-\ell+4}]
. Since our hash function is pairwise-independent, the probability a collision exists in this range is therefore bounded by
\frac{2^{2\ell+8}}{2^{2\ell+2k}}=2^{4-2k}
. Union bounding over the
T_{1}
choices of
H_{1}
,
u
, and
\ell
